#121. Two men found guilty over Iranian attempts to ‘eliminate’ UK-based journalists

Mostafa Sepahvand and Farhad Javadi Manesh carried out surveillance in an effort to inflict violence on targets

Two men of Iranian origin who were hired through social media as part of a plan to “eliminate” journalists in the UK have been found guilty of engaging in conduct likely to assist Tehran’s intelligence services.

Mostafa Sepahvand, 41, and Farhad Javadi Manesh, 46, carried out surveillance and reconnaissance between August 2024 and March last year in an attempt to inflict violence on “subjects” in the UK.

These subjects included two journalists for the London-based Persian broadcaster Iran International, Mojtaba Pourmohsen and Kambiz Hosseini, and a founder member of UK Lawyers for Israel, Jonathan Turner.

The defendants visited addresses linked to their targets, took photos and videos and used platforms such as ChatGPT to research them online. They appear to be part of a growing new tactic used by hostile states in which “proxies” are hired to work for foreign security forces, with often little idea who they are targeting or why.

Last month the Norwegian teenager Johannes Natland, 19, was jailed for life after he agreed to carry out a hit in Huddersfield for the Foxtrot Network, a Swedish organised crime group backed by the Iranian regime.

The Iranian men came to the UK illegally between 2016 and 2022 – Sepahvand in the back of a lorry and Manesh in a small boat.

Sepahvand used the AI platform ChatGPT to find the home address of Pourmohsen, who worked for the London-based Persian language broadcaster Iran International. He hoped to be paid £150,000 to carry out a violent attack and help to “finish off” Pourmohsen.

Sepahvand chatted on WhatsApp to his handler, an Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps commander called Amin, as he spied on Pourmohsen in December 2024, Woolwich crown court heard.

Amin wrote: “These are new pictures of the guy/bloke.”

Referring to a picture, Amin said: “And that dot is their office?”

Sepahvand said: “Love you brother, I’ll go there tomorrow.”

The pair also discussed surveillance of the Iranian satirist Kambiz Hosseini, who eventually had to leave home because of the threats made against him.

Bill Emlyn Jones KC, prosecuting, said Iran and its rulers did not and could tolerate dissent. “One organisation critical to the regime, and therefore very much in its sights, is Iran International, a news organisation which has come under particular attack because of its explicit support for opposition groups and its editorial stance, which is anti-Islamic Republic of Iran.

“Eliminating hostile, critical or dissident actors overseas is a staple policy of the Islamic republic. Together, the defendants were engaged in what is very obviously a form of spying,” Jones said.

Hosseini, 51, had to leave home and stop work as a standup comedian as a result of the threats he received. He was sent a message with a picture of his car saying the next time he started the engine it would blow up. Hosseini’s family in Iran also received threats, and his family had to leave the country.

The court heard Sepahvand had been convicted of theft in Iran and was drawn into the plan to “eliminate” dissidents in the hope of reducing his criminal record.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He told his Iranian handlers he was prepared to escalate from surveillance to violence, the court heard.

He hoped to receive a final payment of £150,000 for “finishing the job”, a reference to killing one of the journalists, the court was told. “If the person [the target] is inside, they pay the £150,000, and I’ll finish him off,” said Sepahvand in a voice note.

Sepahvand claimed his family was being threatened and that he never intended to go through with the hit. He also denied plotting to kill Turner, of UK Lawyers for Israel.

Manesh claimed he took a picture of the Iran International site because it was “a beautiful building” and did not know they were working with the Iranian intelligence services.

Earlier this year, Roman Lavrynovych, 22, and Stanislav Carpiuc, 27, were jailed at the Old Bailey for seven and two years respectively for arson attacks on property connected to Keir Starmer. An anonymous Russian-speaking figure named El Money had recruited Lavrynovych and Carpiuc on Telegram.

Last year, six Britons were jailed for their parts in a Russian-ordered arson attack on a London warehouse providing aid to Ukraine. The ringleader, Dylan Earl, was recruited by the Wagner group, a mercenary organisation that acts on behalf of the Russian state.

Sepahvand was convicted by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct. Manesh was convicted of assisting a foreign intelligence service, but cleared of preparatory conduct.

Shapoor Noori, 57, of Ealing, was cleared by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct.

Mrs Justice Cheema-Grubb remanded Sepahvand and Manesh ahead of sentencing at the Old Bailey on 17 December.

#119. 'My darling girl, you are everything to me' - auctioned letters reveal WWI love story

Gunner Willie Webber sent his new wife Gladys more than 100 letters from the Western Front over 10 months

"My darling girl, you are everything to me..."

Those words were written by Gunner Willie Webber to his wife Gladys from the battlefields of World War One.

The newly-weds, who were married at St Edward's Church in Knighton, Powys, in December 1917, had very little time together as Willie - a gunner with the Royal Garrison Artillery's 297th Siege Battery - was serving on the Western Front. He was killed just weeks before the Armistice in 1918.

More than a century later, Willie's letters have inspired an extraordinary act of remembrance by the community - thanks to the efforts of two strangers who refused to let the couple's love story be forgotten.

The inscription on the memorial bench at St Edward's Church in Knighton

The bundle of 100 letters, dated from December 1917 to October 1918, chart a love story unfolding against the backdrop of war.

After briefly returning home injured and marrying Gladys in late 1917, Willie went back to the front line in France.

He sent his new wife pressed flowers and affectionate messages, signing off: "Your own true hubby & loving sweetheart. Willie."

Willie wrote regularly and his letters are filled with hopes and dreams for the future.

But the reunion he longed for never came.

Just four weeks before the Armistice, in October 1918, the young soldier was killed in an accident when his gun backfired. He was 25 years old.

The couple had no children, no direct descendants and, as time passed, nobody left to tell their story.

Their story could so easily have ended there. Instead, those letters triggered a remarkable act of remembrance more than a century later.

A drawing of Willie was found among the letters, captioned "Gazing Homewards"

In 2025, the collection of love letters was put up for sale at an online auction.

Among those following the auction was Andy Garner, a mechanic from Norwich, who collects, transcribes and self-publishes wartime letters - ensuring the people in them are not lost to history.

When he came across Willie and Gladys' correspondence, he was determined to save it.

"Those letters just kept drawing me back," he said. "I'm adamant Willie was going, 'Come on Andy, you can do this'!'"

The auction price kept climbing, but - in the final seconds - Andy placed a winning bid of Â£351.51.

Later, reading through the complete collection, he found himself deeply moved by the couple's devotion and Willie's steadfast belief he would make it home.

"No matter what he's going through, their love shines through," said Andy.

"It's giving them both the hope to survive."

Though he could barely afford the money he had spent, Andy was determined to publish the transcripts.

Keith Budgen, the man who had sold the letters, decided he couldn't keep the auction money.

"I woke up the following morning and immediately contacted Andy," said Keith.

"I said, 'You can have the money back. It's the right thing to do.'

"I had been given the letters, and I just knew that Andy had to have them."

The pair bonded over the letters and came up with the idea to commemorate Willie, the fallen soldier, in Knighton - the place where he and Gladys had married.

What followed was a remarkable community effort.

Within just two-and-a-half weeks, supporters raised Â£1,600 for a memorial bench at St Edward's Church.

The church immediately embraced the project and a remembrance service was organised. Bell-ringers created a special tribute, a bugler volunteered to play, and members of the Royal British Legion lent their support.

"The fact that he wasn't commemorated on any cenotaph, either in the town where he was born or in Knighton, made it a fabulous idea," said Ian Ferguson, of the Royal British Legion.

Reverend Rachael Storer, of St Edward's Church, paid tribute to Andy's determination to share the couple's story.

"It all started with those letters that he bought at auction," she said.

âUltimately, this is a story about love."

The community gathered at the church where Willie and Gladys married to commemorate their story

Lynn Scales, who wrote a special standard for the bell-ringers in memory of the couple said: "Sometimes we forget the sacrifice that some people have made for the defence of this country.

"Gunner Willie sounds like he was doomed to be forgotten - but not any more."

Andy's wife, Lex, wrote to the BBC because she wanted others to know about her husband's work.

"Once he spreads the word, other people come on board and they all want to help because they care," she said.

"It seems to have this really beautiful ripple effect.

"Willie and Gladys were just regular everyday people, and for someone to actually take that time to remember them, I think it's beautiful."

More than a century after Willie wrote those letters, he and Gladys have found a new community. Not through blood or family ties, but through an act of remembrance initiated by two strangers, who refused to let their story be forgotten.

#118. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#117. Court in UK Decriminalizes Same-Sex Relations in Trinidad and Tobago

The ruling, which could affect similar laws in other British Commonwealth countries, has spotlighted a legal system still intertwined with a former colonial power.

Prior Beharry reported from Port of Spain, Trinidad and Tobago.

A ruling by a British-based court has invalidated laws criminalizing homosexuality in Trinidad and Tobago, setting a precedent for those challenging similar laws in other nations in the Caribbean and possibly beyond.

The ruling was handed down on Tuesday by the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council in London, which serves as the highest court of appeal for dozens of crown dependencies, overseas territories and former British colonies.

Five British judges unanimously upheld a 2018 decision by a lower court in Trinidad that had found two sections of the Caribbean country’s 1986 law targeting private, consensual same-sex relations to be unconstitutional. An appeals court in Trinidad had reversed the lower-court decision in 2025.

Trinidad’s attorney general had argued that the law preserved traditional family values in the country, and had leaned on a legal mechanism called “savings provisions,” which kept many laws from the British colonial era.

The decision delivered a hard-fought victory to advocates for L.G.B.T.Q. rights in Trinidad. Jason Jones, an L.G.B.T.Q. activist who had been a key plaintiff against the law since 2015, called the ruling a “vindication.” He said it would provide hope to young L.G.B.T.Q. people in his country who are living in fear of legal and societal discrimination.

Still, Mr. Jones noted ruefully, to challenge the colonial-era law, his lawyers had to seek intervention from a court based in Britain — decades after Trinidad gained independence, in 1962.

#116. New Indian mega library rocked by scandal over 'AI-generated books'

The newly opened Periyar Arivulagam library is at the centre of the allegations

An Indian court has ordered an investigation into allegations that a public library in the southern state of Tamil Nadu bought hundreds of books suspected of being generated using AI.

The controversy began after Tamil novelist Perumal Murugan raised concerns about books containing false information and questionable authorship at a newly opened library in Coimbatore city.

The Madras High Court has ordered the state's anti-corruption agency to investigate the purchases and freeze bank accounts linked to a publisher accused of supplying the books.

The state government has blamed the previous Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam (DMK) party-led administration for buying the books. The DMK denies allegations.

Tamil Nadu's School Education Minister A Rajmohan has described the allegations as potentially "India's first AI scam" and said the investigation would cover purchases at other public libraries across the state.

The case has raised wider questions about the growing use of AI in publishing, where tools can generate entire books quickly and cheaply, but also produce false information and invented references.

It has also highlighted the challenges facing libraries and publishers in checking the authenticity of books as AI-generated content becomes more widespread.

The controversy centres on the Periyar Arivulagam library, built at a cost of about 3bn rupees ($34m; Â£26m). Named after social reformer Periyar EV Ramasamy, it was inaugurated on 16 October by Tamil Nadu Chief Minister C Joseph Vijay.

Murugan, whose novels Pyre and One Part Woman have received international recognition, raised concerns after visiting the library last month.

Writing in an online magazine, he alleged that nearly 1,800 books in its Tamil section had been supplied by a little-known publisher called Agal Vilakku Pathippagam.

He said many of the books carried unfamiliar authors' names, unusually high prices and questionable International Standard Book Numbers (ISBNs).

One book cited in the controversy, Naladiyar Vaazhvin Vazhikatti (A Guide to Life through Naladiyar), was reportedly priced at 1,445 rupees for 300 pages.

It describes Naladiyar as a Tamil poet and philosopher, although Naladiyar is a classical Tamil work consisting of 400 four-line verses on morality and ethics.

Another book reportedly attributes the ancient Tamil poem Madurai Kanchi to an incorrect author. It remains unclear whether AI was used to produce any of the disputed books.

The library was inaugurated by Chief Minister C Joseph Vijay

Questions have also been raised about a book found at another public library in Madurai which names renowned economist Jean DrÃ¨ze as its author.

The book, Democracy and People's Rights in the Contemporary World, names renowned economist Jean DrÃ¨ze as its author and was published by Phoenix Publishing House.

DrÃ¨ze did not respond to BBC Tamil's request for comment but told The Hindu newspaper earlier this week that he had not written the book, raising concerns that his name may have been used without his permission.

BBC Tamil visited a Chennai address listed by Agal Vilakku and another publisher named in the controversy, Phoenix Publishing House, but found no signs identifying either business.

A woman at the address confirmed that Agal Vilakku operated there. Its proprietor, Mohan, declined to comment, saying the matter was before the courts.

The controversy has prompted writers, academics and publishers to question how the books were selected for public libraries.

More than 160 writers, researchers, artists and publishers have signed a joint statement calling for an independent inquiry, the release of procurement records and an audit of library collections across Tamil Nadu.

They have also called for the recovery of public money if the investigation finds that funds were misused.

The group questioned how the disputed books could have passed through a selection process involving expert committees, price negotiations and several levels of review.

The state government has ordered an internal audit of public library collections.

Rajmohan said 523 books had already been removed from the historic Connemara Public Library in Chennai city after preliminary checks raised concerns about their authenticity and quality.

Disputed books have also been removed from Periyar Arivulagam, while anti-corruption officials examine procurement records and inspect other libraries, he added.

The controversy also sparked a political row in Tamil Nadu. Rajmohan accused the previous DMK government of ordering the disputed books and said the state would seek to recover public money if the investigation found wrongdoing.

The DMK has rejected the allegations as politically motivated and has called for an investigation into the purchases.

#114. NAZA: how and where to watch the Guardian’s acclaimed Gaza documentary

Film features testimony from 24 Israeli officials involved in surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians

NAZA will be released in full on the Guardian’s website for free in early December. Sign up here to be notified of the exact streaming date and to be sent a link to the film when it is live.

NAZA has been shown by cinemas in 47 countries and seen by more than 1.5 million people since its release last month, making it one of the most-watched documentary films with a theatrical release in 2026. The film has been offered to a number of distributors and cinemas in Israel but has not been screened there yet.

NAZA is a documentary featuring testimony from 24 Israeli military intelligence officers and soldiers involved in the surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians during the war in Gaza. The film’s title comes from an Israeli military acronym used euphemistically to refer to civilians expected to be killed in a military attack.

The interviewees detail the methods used by the Israeli military in Gaza, including AI-based systems built to identify large numbers of potential Hamas operatives, who were then bombed in their homes at night, in the knowledge that the attacks would also kill their families.

Interviews were filmed at night on rooftops in Tel Aviv, on the condition of anonymity, because of the risks involved in speaking publicly. The appearance and voices of participants in the film have been digitally altered to disguise their identities.

The film was directed by the Oscar-winning Israeli directors Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor and produced by the Guardian. It builds on revelations first published by the Israeli-Palestinian magazine +972, the Hebrew-language news outlet Local Call and the Guardian newspaper between 2023 and 2025.

Abraham and Szor were two of the four co-directors – with the Palestinians Basel Adra and Hamdan Ballal – of No Other Land, which documents the destruction of Masafer Yatta, a village in the occupied West Bank targeted by Israeli forces. It won the Oscar for best documentary in 2025.

NAZA was co-produced by James Wilson and executive-produced by Jonathan Glazer, the team behind the Oscar-winning film The Zone of Interest.

NAZA has received widespread critical acclaim and is selling out in cinemas across the world. The New York Times says it is “tough to watch and impossible to ignore”, while New York Magazine describes it as “one of the most important films of our time”. In a five-star review, the Financial Times says that “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film”.

The film won the special jury prize at the Venice film festival in September, where its premiere had a 25-minute ovation – a festival record. In November, it won the best political documentary from the Critics Choice Association.

The film, its directors and their confidential sources have faced fierce attacks from the Israeli government and military. The prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, has ordered the attorney general and the head of the Shin Bet security service to reveal the identities of sources interviewed and prosecute them for causing “harm to Israel’s diplomatic and security interests”.

Netanyahu also threatened to strip Abraham and Szor of their citizenship, accusing them of treason. The Israeli military’s chief of staff, Eyal Zamir, said the military would “not allow soldiers to be made targets through lies and blood libels” and would use “every tool at our disposal” against the film.

Far-right groups have vowed to hound the film-makers on their return to Israel and picketed Szor’s family home. Some of the messages included explicit calls for violence, with one protester demanding the “death penalty for traitors”.

The Israeli military claimed there were “substantial errors” in the film and that it had relied on sources who had only a partial picture of the target approval process. It said that while AI was used to help identify targets in Gaza, final decisions on strikes, and the risk of civilian casualties, were signed off by officers.

In response, the team behind the film have revealed they spoke to more than 100 sources with knowledge of the military systems used in the war.

The Israeli government is promoting an online film project, called NAZA: The Real Story, which mimics the style of the genuine NAZA film and is hosted on a website called nazadocumentary.com. It is not the real NAZA documentary and has been misleading some viewers.

Initially, the rival film’s website falsely presented itself as a promotional page for NAZA itself, naming Abraham and Szor as the directors, and listing film festivals where the documentary was to be screened. The site now appears as a platform for the rival film, which has been promoted by Israeli embassies.

Ariel Averbuch, an Israeli attorney and army reservist who co-created NAZA: The Real Story, describes it as an “offensive move” to shift the world’s focus from allegations about the Israeli military’s killing of civilians.

Szor and Abraham have addressed the backlash to their documentary. “Our country is doing so much to hide these systems,” Szor said.

NAZA is in cinemas worldwide and will screen for free on the Guardian’s website from early December. Sign up here to be the first to watch it

#113. ‘The world we know is over’ without urgent climate action, leaders tell Cop31

Marshall Islands president warns of worsening floods as UN chief calls for a windfall tax on oil and gas firms

Failure to provide an overwhelming response to the climate crisis means “the world we know is over”, the president of the embattled Marshall Islands has warned, as dozens of leaders gathered in Turkey for the Cop31 climate summit.

Hilda Heine, one of a handful of Pacific Island leaders to make an impassioned speech at Cop31 on Wednesday, told other heads of state that overshooting the 1.5C Paris temperature target would mean waves eating away their shores and floods that destroy towns, schools and hospitals.

She described seeing bright, lush coral beneath the water of her home island in July that had been bleached white just months later.

“It’s time to wake up,” said Heine. “Our response to the threat of overshoot must be overwhelming, or the world we know is over.”

Mia Mottley, the prime minister of Barbados, also called out governments who had “not tried hard enough” to curb emissions since the Paris agreement.

“The UN tells us that, even if every pledge is delivered, we are heading for 2.6 degrees,” she said. “On current policies, 2.8 degrees is where we will end up.

“Let me be plain: overshoot is not a new target. It is a debt, a debt of heat, borrowed against our children’s future. And it must be repaid.”

The opening of the summit of international leaders, on day three of Cop31, saw impassioned speeches interspersed with vigorous displays of Turkish music and dancing.

António Guterres, the UN secretary general, blamed a deceitful fossil fuel industry and countries that “keep expanding fossil fuel production while claiming to be committed to 1.5C”. His speech – a salvo aimed at boosting the talks – was one of the last Guterres, a vocal climate advocate, will deliver as secretary general before he stands down next month.

“We need an extraordinary acceleration,” Guterres said. “Our planet is heating up. The clock is winding down. It’s make or bake time.

“Fossil fuels must go the way of the dinosaurs,” he added. “And that won’t happen on its own. The dinosaurs did not have a lobby – fossil fuels do.”

Guterres said oil and gas companies had distorted science and delayed action on the climate crisis, and called for a windfall tax on their bumper profits they have made from the Iran war. “It’s time to kick the habit and end the fossil-fuel addiction,” he said, to applause. “It’s time for governments to tax these windfall profits and use them to help vulnerable countries recover. Polluters must pay.”

Unusually, Turkey is co-hosting Cop with Australia, with the leaders of both countries, Recep Tayyip Erdoğan and Anthony Albanese respectively, each praising the partnership.

Erdoğan said he wants Cop31 to be remembered as a turning point in the climate crisis and called on countries to act, rather than just make promises. “Every minute we fail to meet the targets in the Paris agreement, the risks and costs we face continue to grow,” the Turkish president said. However, on Monday, away from the conference hall, ​​Erdoğan celebrated the discovery of two new oilfields with an estimated in-situ potential of 135m barrels.

In her speech before world leaders, Heine said every new oil well drilled and every piece of coal extracted was a threat to her country’s future. Without naming any individual oil or gas producer, she added: “The discovery of new fossil fuel resources is not something to celebrate but something to fear.”

Albanese said the conference was a “historic moment” for Australia. The country is one of the world’s biggest exporters of coal and liquified gas. Albanese said governments had a “solemn responsibility” to act on the climate crisis, but he was criticised for failing to mention fossil fuels in his speech.

The gathering of leaders was relatively threadbare. While 91 national leaders attended, according to the UN, major figures such as Donald Trump of the US and Xi Jinping of China were absent. Perhaps tellingly, some other leaders who declined to attend, including the French president, Emmanuel Macron, and the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, are instead heading to Ireland for a conference on AI.

Almost every country at Cop31 is lagging in cutting emissions, with insufficient finance for the nations most vulnerable to extreme heat, floods, fires and other climate-fuelled calamities.

Although the world is barrelling past the 1.5C limit and is entering the teeth of one of the strongest El Niño events in history, there is little sign that the talks will feature any further progress on phasing out planet-heating fossil fuels.

“I’m getting a feeling of panic from around here, from the science community,” said Bill Hare, a climate scientist and chief executive of Climate Analytics, who described fraught conversations with rainforest and cryosphere scientists. “The mood is gradually darkening among scientists. In fact, people talk about feeling as though things have gone backwards.”

Small island nations face an existential threat from rising sea levels and are some of the countries most exposed to catastrophe when global heating shoots past 1.5C. The UN Environment Programme has found that such overshoot is now inevitable, and warned that its effects on icy regions will be so calamitous it should be kept as brief and low as possible.

Other world leaders stressed the urgency of this moment of multiple crises and the need for multilateralism to finally deliver. Gordana Siljanovska-Davkova, the president of North Macedonia, said: “We are the first generation to fully understand the consequences of our choices – and perhaps the last with time to change course. Future generations will live with the consequences of our decisions.”

Nataša Pirc Musar, the president of Slovenia, said: “If we cannot agree that human dignity and respect for nature must be at the heart of our decisions then, frankly, what is left to unite us?”

#112. 'It can kill us': Kenyans fear impact of El Niño with floods predicted

Agnes Shikoli says she fears either drowning, or being crushed by her house

"It flooded up to here in March," Agnes Shikoli says, pointing to a spot halfway up the 6ft [1.8m] corrugated iron wall of her home in Kibera, one of the largest informal settlements in Kenya.

Her tiny house lies just a couple of metres from the Ngong river, which runs through this densely populated area in the capital, Nairobi.

The scars of floods in the long rainy season earlier this year can still be seen - including a broken TV in the corner which was immersed in water.

Tucked out of sight is a photo of her sister Beatrice Khalayi, 58, who died when intense rains caused widespread destruction in Kibera. She was among more than 60 people across the country who lost their lives.

"Someone called me to tell me that she had died of electrocution after her house flooded," Shikoli recalls. "When I see a picture of her, I feel uneasy, that's why I keep it far from me, so I don't see it."

With Kenya's short rainy season now starting, Shikoli tells the BBC she is afraid - especially following warnings that the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon could bring a new round of flooding.

"I feel like it can kill us. These houses aren't made of stone," says the 62-year-old. "They can fall on us, or the water can drown us."

Kibera - and Kenya more widely - are on the front line of preparations for the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

Forecasters have warned that this year could see one of the strongest on record, bringing extreme weather to many parts of the world.

In southern Kenya, that means the threat of rains even more intense than usual.

Outside Shikoli's house, groups of men and women wearing high-visibility vests are shovelling and raking garbage out of the riverbed and disposing it far from the water.

This programme called Climate Worx, under the office of President William Ruto, is meant to rehabilitate Nairobi's rivers. Such exercises have intensified since August when warnings increased about the possible scale of this year's El NiÃ±o.

Rubbish clogging Nairobi's drains and sewers was seen as a major factor in the deadly floods earlier this year

Similar work to unclog drainage systems is ongoing across Nairobi, a sprawling city built on hilly ground crossed by three major rivers.

But the workers have a huge task on their hands.

Blocked drains and inadequate sewage systems were widely blamed for the high number of deaths after the floods earlier in the year, as the excess rainfall had nowhere to escape.

The Nairobi County disaster management chief, Bramwell Simiyu tells the BBC that drainage systems have been "untouched for many years" and that removing the rubbish from them will save lives.

Floods disproportionately affect informal settlements in Nairobi, especially those near rivers, like Kibera and Mathare.

In most of these settlements, houses sit just a few metres from rivers. Some people, including Shikoli, live on the slopes of the river itself due to the constant expansion of the settlements. When flash floods occur, these houses are swept away, endangering the lives of their occupants.

Nairobi's drainage system has not been upgraded to deal with the rapid growth in the population and infrastructure over the decades

"The existing drainage system was developed 30 years ago to cater for a population of about 2.5 million residents but we're dealing with about seven million in the daytime and 5.5 million night-time," Simiyu says.

He adds that investment of up to $192m (Â£143m) is needed to upgrade the sewerage and drainage system entirely.

But there is little sign that either the cash-strapped national or city council will able to raise this kind of money.

The Nairobi River Basin Regeneration programme, a government programme aimed at restoring the city's rivers, found that settlements near rivers, and where there is weak enforcement of regulations, have increased flooding risks. It recommended a "buffer" to protect rivers and "humane relocation" of communities where necessary.

However, rising poverty has driven many people to live close to rivers.

"Our worst case scenario looking at low-income settlements is that up to about 100,000 people are at risk," Simiyu says. "Moderate projections is about 5,000 households or about 20,000 people may be at risk."

Anyone forced to leave their homes will be relocated to 167 designated holding grounds where a stockpile of relief food and supplies will be distributed, Simiyu says.

Nobody knows exactly when the rains will come - or how much rain there will be.

But Kenya's meteorological department warns that November is usually the peak rainfall month of the short rainy season, which normally lasts from October to December.

A regional forecast from the Intergovernmental Authority on Development (Igad) warned in September that there was "an 81% probability of a very strong El NiÃ±o during October-December 2026".

So far, the heavy rains predicted have not occurred but experts remain concerned.

In East Africa, this El NiÃ±o is being widely compared to a similar weather event in 1997-1998 which caused widespread flooding and destruction in Somalia and Kenya.

Weather forecaster Jemima Gacheru tells the BBC that is because temperature conditions in the Indian Ocean in the 1997 El NiÃ±o and those recorded now are similar. This interaction, she says, has been seen to result in very strong rainfall.

"For this season we have a very strong El NiÃ±o already in play in the Pacific and then we have a positive Indian Ocean dipole," Gacheru said. This is often called the "Indian NiÃ±o" because of its similarity to its Pacific equivalent and refers to the difference in sea-surface temperatures in opposite parts of the Indian Ocean.

"We expect to have some enhanced rainfall over several parts of the country."

The UN's migration body the International Organization for Migration (IOM) has warned that almost 900,000 people could be forced to leave their homes in Kenya this year, mostly because of the predicted floods.

Dozens of people died in floods in Kenya earlier this year

Kenya is not the only African country worried about the potential impact of El NiÃ±o.

In neighbouring Somalia, the country's disaster management agency has warned that 1.2 million people are at risk of displacement caused by extreme weather - including the risk of both floods and drought.

The 1998 floods killed 2,000 people in Somalia and forced a further 250,000 to flee their homes, according to the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). Up to 80% of crops were destroyed in some parts of the country.

The United Nation's famine warning system IPC has warned of the increased risk of food insecurity in Somalia due to supply chain issues exacerbated by the impact of El NiÃ±o.

Elsewhere on the continent forecasters fear El NiÃ±o could bring extreme heat and prolonged periods without rain.

It could cause a crisis for farmers across parts of West Africa, including the semi-arid Sahel region south of the Sahara, as well as in southern parts of the continent, where the risk of crop failure is high.

There are fears El NiÃ±o's impact will worsen existing issues.

Communities like Kibera are already grappling with poor housing and rampant poverty, while parts of Somalia are in the middle of a devastating drought.

National governments in many countries are doing what they can to prepare but the extent of El NiÃ±o's impact is impossible to predict.

Back in Kibera, Shikoli and her family are preparing for the worst.

Council officials have told them they should leave their home on the riverbank until the rainy season has passed but Shikoli makes just $2 a day selling groundnuts and fruit from a small stall next to the river.

"I want to move," she says. "But I don't have the means.

"We just have to persevere and leave it to God."

#111. Pope Leo XIV Goes Home to Peru, Meeting Ghosts of His Past

In Peru, where Leo XIV lived for two decades and became a citizen, the pope was met by President Keiko Fujimori, whose father’s policies he had protested.

Motoko Rich, the Rome bureau chief, is traveling with Pope Leo XIV during his tour of Uruguay, Argentina and Peru. Mitra Taj reported from Lima, Peru.

Just a year and a half into his papacy and less than a month after surgeons removed a lobe of his right lung, Pope Leo XIV, the first pontiff born in the United States, touched down in his homeland on Wednesday.

Yet instead of setting foot on American soil, he arrived in Peru, where he spent two decades as a missionary, priest and bishop and became a citizen in 2015.

“Today I understand how much of this land remains with me and how much of my own story also belongs to you,” Leo said, speaking on Wednesday evening to Peru’s president, civic leaders and diplomats at the Gran Teatro Nacional, a theater and concert hall in the country’s capital, Lima. One of his biggest applause lines was when he referred to Peru as “my homeland.”

Having turned out record crowds at Masses in Argentina, the pope flew from Buenos Aires to Lima on Wednesday afternoon, reaching the final country of his 12-day tour of South America. The pope emerged at the top of the stairs next to a sign on the side of his plane that read “Pontifex Maximus,” and was greeted by Peru’s president, Keiko Fujimori, and a chorus of singing children on a red carpet at the Callao Air Base near Lima.

As his motorcade made its way through city streets under gray skies, about 3 million people lined the roads, according to local authorities, holding up cellphones as they waited for a glimpse of the pontiff.

His choice to visit Peru so soon after becoming pope — while deferring a visit to the country of his birth — underscored the deep emotional ties he feels to the place that formed him as a priest.

#110. Bundeswehr study exposes widespread sexual harassment in German military

Sexual misconduct in the Bundeswehr is far more widespread than previously known, with younger women disproportionately affected. Defense Minister Boris Pistorius has called for a policy of "zero tolerance."

"It was clear to us from the outset that the analysis would uncover uncomfortable truths," Defence Minister Boris Pistorius of the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD) told theBundestag, Germany's lower house of parliament, on Wednesday.

Earlier that day, he had presented the findings of the Bundeswehr's first study into unreported cases of sexual misconduct to the Defense Committee. According to the findings, sexual misconduct in the Bundeswehr is far more widespread than previously known.

"The study has shown that only between one third and one sixth of cases are reported," Pistorius said, announcing an action plan to address the issue.

"There is zero tolerance for sexual misconduct in the Bundeswehr. This is nothing less than a matter of upholding the human dignity enshrined in our Basic Law. And that principle applies all the more within the Bundeswehr."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The study examined incidents that had gone unreported, the so-called "dark figure" of cases that never reach official statistics. The Bundeswehr Centre of Military History and Social Sciences (ZMSBw) surveyed 45,000 service members, around 18% of whom responded. More than 8,100 questionnaires were evaluated and supplemented with individual and group interviews. According to the institute, the data are statistically representative.

According to the study, younger women in the lower ranks are particularly affected by sexual misconduct. Among men, non-heterosexual soldiers are disproportionately affected. The perpetrators are predominantly male. Statistically, combat units do not differ from the rest of the armed forces.

Female service members in units with a higher proportion of women (more than 15%) are less likely to be affected.

Verbal harassment is by far the most common form of misconduct, according to the study. Around 72% of female soldiers and 31% of male soldiers said they had experienced verbal harassment at least once during their military service. In the 12 months preceding the survey, 38% of female soldiers and 16% of male soldiers reported experiencing verbal sexual harassment.

For physical harassment, 9% of female soldiers and 3% of male soldiers reported incidents of physical sexual misconduct, and 2% of both women and men said they had experienced sexual violence.

The study also examined why service members chose not to report incidents. The most commonly cited reason was not wanting to draw attention to themselves.

"There appears to be a lack of awareness of the reporting channels. And, for me, the decisive issue is trust in those channels," Pistorius concluded.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In response, the Bundeswehr plans to improve awareness of how and where cases of sexual harassment can be reported. That is precisely where the environmentalist Greens, who are in opposition in the Bundestag, direct their criticism.

Defense policy spokesperson Sara Nanni is calling for "an external reporting office, alongside the regular disciplinary process, with investigative powers and the authority to take disciplinary action where appropriate." Such an independent point of contact, she argues, is needed in cases where superiors within the Bundeswehr fail to fulfill their responsibilities.

Another consequence of the study is the expansion of support services for those affected. A counseling center established in early November will soon be available around the clock. Supervisors are also to receive enhanced awareness training and improved instruction.

"Sexual misconduct will become part of training at every level, from the very beginning," said Bundeswehr Inspector General Carsten Breuer.

In addition, the way supervisors handle reported cases will become a standing criterion in their performance evaluations. "Anyone who fails to lead by example, tolerates misconduct, or does not address it consistently will no longer hold a leadership position," Defense Minister Pistorius stressed.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The study was prompted by a growing number of reports of sexual misconduct received by former Parliamentary Commissioner for the Armed Forces Eva Högl. Most of those who approached her were female soldiers. Högl called for the study into unreported cases in 2024. The process gained further momentum following incidents at the Parachute Regiment in Zweibrücken that came to light at the beginning of this year and also involved allegations of sexual misconduct.

The current Parliamentary Commissioner for the Armed Forces, Henning Otte, described the figures revealed by the study as "dramatically high." The findings had also made it clear, he said, "that there is a lack of trust in the Bundeswehr's structures. The Ministry of Defense must now take this study seriously and translate its findings into a leadership culture that is truly lived in practice."

The Bundeswehr urgently needs additional personnel and has been actively recruiting women. However, the study's findings are unlikely to make attracting more female recruits any easier. Women remain significantly underrepresented across nearly all branches of the armed forces.

This article was originally written in German.

#109. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#108. ‘Like priests who don’t believe in God’: Italy’s anti-vax medics eye return to frontline duty

Progress of bill to reinstate struck-off doctors hinged on support from far right figure Roberto Vannacci, whose rise is shaping politics before 2027 elections

Antonio Cremasco contracted Covid at a dinner party on 6 March 2020. Four days later, as Italy became the first European country to go into nationwide lockdown, he was fighting for his life. “I spent 20 days in intensive care, followed by another 20 days in a hospital ward,” said Cremasco, from Bassano del Grappa in the northern Italian Veneto region.

After leaving hospital, he needed oxygen therapy for another six months. Cremasco’s ordeal was such that when Covid vaccines became available in Italy in early 2021, he had no qualms about being vaccinated, and he believes the decision saved his life once again.

“All those who had been in intensive care with me died,” he said. “Without the vaccine, if I had caught Covid again, I had a high risk of dying too.”

But Cremasco, who is now 76 and recently received his annual Covid and winter flu jab, has struggled to convince his vaccine-sceptic son, a father of two, of the drugs’ safety. He said he and his wife were wary of having their potentially unvaccinated grandchildren in their home whenever the children were ill, fearing for their own health. “They always seem to be sick,” Cremasco said.

His family rift mirrors a wider divide over vaccination in Italy, which has been brought back to the political spotlight by a controversial bill approved in October in the lower house of parliament.

The measure allows healthcare workers who were struck off their professional registers during the pandemic for serious violations connected to their anti-vaccination beliefs – for example, promoting treatments that lacked a scientific basis – to apply to be reinstated.

The bill is being championed by the prime minister Giorgia Meloni’s Brothers of Italy party and is strongly backed by its far-right coalition partner, the League. But with allies in Forza Italia split, its approval in the lower house hinged on support from National Future (FN), a rival far-right force led by the former army general Roberto Vannacci, whose emergence is shaping the political landscape before general elections next year. Vannacci has voiced opposition to vaccine mandates, and his rhetoric frequently targets the vaccine-sceptic audience.

The measure still needs to clear the senate, with a vote scheduled in January. But its progress has alarmed medical experts, less because of the number of healthcare workers involved – it is believed to be very small – and more because of the risk of further eroding trust in vaccines.

Nearly one in two Italian adults are hesitant about vaccination, according to a report by the department of public health and paediatric sciences at the University of Turin published in the Lancet Regional Health Europe in February. The report, which sampled 50,000 adults across Italy, found the ambivalence was driven more by misinformation and lack of institutional trust than by fears over vaccine safety.

“Political communication and the lack of trust among Italians in institutions carry a significant weight,” said Roberta Siliquini, the head of the school of hygiene and preventive medicine at the University of Turin and the report’s chief researcher. “There really is a disinformation campaign in this sense – and those who want to disinform are very good at communicating.”

The implications are being profoundly felt: this year there has been a resurgence in measles, with 472 infections reported during the first five months, compared with 322 in the same period last year, according to health ministry figures. More than half of the cases were reported in young adults. Take-up of the flu vaccine among people aged 65 and over so far this season has dipped to 49.81%, the first time it has fallen below 50% since the winter of 2014-15.

In August, a four-year-old child in Palermo, Sicily, died of diphtheria. Her parents reportedly told doctors they had not vaccinated her because her cousin was autistic and they were convinced vaccines were the reason. There is no evidence of any link between autism and vaccines.

Siquilini said the tragic case was a reminder of the responsibility that everyone, “but especially politicians”, had to deliver the correct message on vaccines. She warned that the rehabilitation of those who rejected scientific evidence could have a “devastating” impact on how people perceived vaccines.

The message has been repeatedly undermined. On 28 October, a day after the lower house vote, Claudio Borghi, a League politician, came under fire for hosting an anti-vaccine seminar in the senate.

Later in the day, in what was believed to have been an indirect rebuke, the Italian president, Sergio Mattarella used a speech during a high-profile cancer research gathering to condemn the spread of anti-vax messages while warning against “irrational beliefs” and “destructive fake news”.

Borghi has proposed revising a law introduced in 2017 by the then leftwing government that extended the number of obligatory childhood vaccinations from four to 10, adding, for example, one against pertussis, or whooping cough. He has defended his position, arguing he is not anti-vaccine but that mandatory vaccines “lead to resistance”.

As for the reinstatement of the medics struck off during the pandemic, he told the Guardian that the proposal’s aim was “to foster reconciliation after all these years”. He said: “In our view, nobody should lose their job because of a personal belief.”

Scepticism over vaccinations took root in Italy in the late 1990s, as in many other countries, mainly driven by now debunked claims of a connection between the combined measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccination and autism.

The arrival of social media accelerated the spread of vaccine misinformation, contributing to a drop in immunisation rates against preventable diseases. The 2017 move to make childhood vaccinations obligatory further emboldened anti-vaccine groups while creating fertile ground for rightwing populist parties, including the Five Star Movement (M5S), which continued to spread misinformation about vaccines after coming to power in 2018, and before the realities of the pandemic forced a shift in tone. Now in opposition, M5S is highly critical of the move to re-embrace anti-vax healthcare workers.

Lorenzo Mattia Signori, a nurse in Veneto, said politicians were “opportunists” and Meloni’s coalition was simply trying to draw votes from anti-vax voters as it eyed a second mandate.

Signori, who is also a regional secretary for Cittadinanzattiva, an association focused on patient advocacy, including pushing for campaigns to inform citizens about vaccine benefits, said: “Ensuring that people get vaccinated is the largest prevention system we have. If everyone gets sick instead, the Italian healthcare system would not hold.”

He hopes that Mattarella will use his power as president to stop the anti-vaccine medics measure from being signed into law. “We cannot have them in hospitals,” he said. “It would be like going to church and hearing a priest who says he doesn’t believe in God.”

#107. Can Nigeria's president regain voters' trust after painful first term?

President Tinubu faces re-election with the cost of living and economic hardship the major issues for many voters

Nigeria's President Bola Ahmed Tinubu has launched his re-election manifesto, promising to grow the economy, tackle insecurity and address housing shortages.

The plan targets 6% annual economic growth by 2031, 78,000 additional security personnel and 200,000 affordable homes.

Titled From Reform to Prosperity, the manifesto builds on Tinubu's first-term economic reforms, including the removal of the fuel subsidy, which has pushed up living costs.

Nigeria is due to hold a general election in January 2027, with Peter Obi and former Vice-President Atiku Abubakar among Tinubu's main challengers.

The manifesto, launched on 10 November by his party, the All Progressives Congress (APC), reflects the government's bid to win back the trust of Nigerians.

Elected in 2023, Tinubu undertook a series of economic reforms, including the removal of fuel subsidies, the harmonisation of official and parallel foreign exchange rates, and tax reforms.

"That manifesto was trying to get Nigeria back on its feet, given the condition of the country at that time, slipping into austerity," Joshua Osiyemi, who heads Tracka, a platform launched by the BudgIT Foundation, which is a non-profit organisation that aims to make government financial data easier to understand.

The reforms, although considered necessary by some economists, have been painful for many Nigerians.

"Fuel prices went up, and everything that Nigerians consume skyrocketed," Osiyemi told the BBC.

"Most Nigerian families are saying they cannot survive on 10,000 naira [$7.40; Â£5.50] a day," he said, referring to a survey conducted by his organisation.

Analysis by Nigeria's International Centre for Investigative Reporting (ICIR) found that the price of imported rice rose by 184% between May 2023, when Tinubu took office, and October 2025. Other staples, including beans, garri (cassava flour) and bread, also recorded substantial increases.

In a sign that the reforms might be starting to pay off, SBM Intelligence, a security risk consulting firm, found that the cost of preparing a pot of jollof rice fell by 11.5% between June and September 2026, the sharpest decline it had recorded in the 10 years it has been running its Jollof Index.

However, this has not translated into significant relief for most households.

"Nigerians spend more and more of their income on basics," said Ikenna Okonkwo, head of research at SBM Intelligence.

The reforms were praised by the World Bank and the International Monetary Fund (IMF) for their effectiveness.

Nigeria's headline inflation stood at 14% in September 2026, according to the National Bureau of Statistics, down from a high of 34% in 2024. The gap between the official and parallel exchange rates has also narrowed.

Tinubu has said the removal of fuel subsidies freed up more than one trillion ($740m; Â£550m) naira in government resources in just the first two months.

Nigeria is due to hold elections in January, with Peter Obi and former Vice-President Atiku Abubakar (pictured above) among Tinubu's main opposition challengers

Following widespread complaints about the rising cost of living, he recently said he would work to reduce the cost of fuel but denied that this was a return to the subsidy. For decades, many Nigerians have seen cheap fuel as one of the few perks of living in Africa's largest oil producer.

His administration subsequently announced a 125bn naira intervention package for small businesses, including grants for smaller enterprises and loans for manufacturers.

Atiku has criticised Tinubu's decision to remove the fuel subsidy.

"My position stands! I will restore subsidy to make life easier and affordable for Nigerians," Atiku wrote on X.

However, restoring the fuel subsidy could prove challenging given Nigeria's economic realities.

"The current budget for Nigeria in 2026 is about 68.32tn naira, with 15.8tn going towards debt servicing. So, we are not fiscally strong enough to say that we want to return to the subsidy," Osiyemi said.

While Tinubu's reforms are lauded for restoring some stability at the macroeconomic level, they have also created instability in households.

Two months away from elections, the cost of living and economic hardship are the major issues for many voters.

"These kinds of things can get punished in polls," said Okonkwo of SBM Intelligence.

To address this, Tinubu promised to lower the cost of living, grow the economy by 6% and halve youth unemployment if he wins re-election. Achieving these targets will require increased oil and agricultural production.

But much of Nigeria's agricultural production is concentrated in the north, where insecurity has made farming increasingly difficult.

Osiyemi says many farmers are reluctant to return to their fields.

"They're not sure whether when they go to their farms, they have the chances of also coming back home, to meet with their family."

To address insecurity, Tinubu proposes recruiting an additional 28,000 military personnel and 50,000 police officers.

"The security situation, especially in the north, is more complex than a matter of just recruiting more soldiers," Okonkwo said.

The kidnap gangs, jihadists and separatists wreaking havoc in Nigeria

'We fear for our souls' - Nigerian farmers need armed guards to protect them from jihadists

Why Nigeria's one-time political giant may face extinction

The threats include jihadists, kidnap gangs and conflict between herders and farmers in central Nigeria.

Insecurity is also a major priority for Obi, the Nigeria Democratic Congress (NDC) presidential candidate, who came third in the 2023 presidential election and attracted significant support from young Nigerians.

He unveiled his 2027 Governance Agenda on 10 November, under the campaign slogan "A New Nigeria is Possible", placing national unity at the top of his priorities, followed by security.

"Nobody will kidnap school children and disappear into the bush. If you kidnap children and go into the bush, you will not be alone there. We will come after you," he said.

Further, delivering on those ambitions will also depend on improving electricity supply, particularly for businesses and industries. Tinubu promises to increase available electricity generation towards 10,000MW.

"There is nothing in the manifesto about where that investment is going to come from because it will cost a lot of money," said Okonkwo.

Unreliable electricity supplies force many households and businesses to rely on generators, pushing up the cost of living

Another huge challenge facing Nigeria is the power sector, which has suffered from years of underinvestment.

The Nigerian Electricity Regulatory Commission says the country had installed generation capacity of 13,625MW at the end of 2024, but generated an average of just 4,223MW during the year.

Nigeria's power sector has failed to produce at capacity due to ageing infrastructure, inadequate maintenance, gas supply constraints and funding challenges.

The government has also privatised electricity distribution, but distribution companies have struggled to invest in infrastructure, including local transformers and metering systems, partly because of low profit margins.

"The government cannot increase tariffs to an extent where people will revolt, and that also keeps investors away because they don't look at the Nigerian power sector as somewhere that they can make money," said Okonkwo.

Unreliable electricity supplies force many households and businesses to rely on generators, pushing up the cost of living and doing business.

The manifesto also promises to build 200,000 affordable homes. This is a fraction of Nigeria's estimated housing deficit, which a technical committee set up by the housing ministry put at 14.9 million units in 2025.

For Nigerians to feel a meaningful improvement in their lives, the reforms will need to go beyond the promises outlined in the manifesto, analysts say.

"Some of the items in the manifesto seem bloated, but if they can be committed to it, I want to believe that quite a number of these items that they have here are very much achievable," Osiyemi said.

"The big thing, if the APC wins, which they have to do and is a tall order, is to regain the trust of Nigerians," Okonkwo said.

Nigeria's 2023 presidential election saw Tinubu declared winner with 8.79 million votes, ahead of Atiku and Obi. Tinubu's victory came amid growing economic hardship after eight years of Muhammadu Buhari's APC government.

"APC were able to win because opposition was divided between two very strong candidates with two regional bases. In the end, we still go down to basic questions like the region you are from and tribal sentiments," Okonkwo said.

As Nigeria heads towards the 2027 elections, Tinubu, Atiku and Obi are once again central to the contest, but the opposition faces a familiar challenge - building a united front and support beyond regional strongholds.

"It will play out like it did in 2023," Okonkwo said.

"The APC are in a stronger position being the incumbent party and the momentum that some of the opposition candidates had in 2023 has faded."

President Tinubu to 'pray for Nigeria' during Saudi Arabia trip

The Interview: Peter Obi, Nigerian presidential candidate

#105. Strike on Gaza Hospital Kills at Least 6 and Injures 20, Rescuers Say

The Israeli authorities said that Hamas had established a command center at the hospital complex. Hamas said the attack was “a heinous crime targeting a civilian medical facility.”

The Israeli military struck a hospital in the southern Gaza Strip on Thursday, killing at least six people and injuring 28 more, according to local health officials.

Dr. Ahmed al-Farra, general director of Nasser Hospital, said in a news conference on Thursday that two missiles had hit the facility and that among the dead was Rezeq Abu Shaira, 52, a doctor.

Health officials reached in Gaza by phone said that a man called Khaled Abu Anza had also been killed, though his affiliation was not immediately clear.

The Israeli security agency and military said in a joint statement on Thursday that they had targeted Muhammad Abu Ismail, whom they identified as a Hamas commander “responsible for holding numerous hostages captive,” during the Gaza war.

The Israeli authorities said that Hamas had established a command center at the hospital complex to stage operations against Israeli forces and was storing weapons there. They added that steps had been taken to mitigate harm to civilians.

Hamas said the Israeli claim that the hospital was used for military activity was a “blatant attempt to justify a heinous crime targeting a civilian medical facility.”

In a statement, Gaza’s health ministry condemned the attack, calling it a “flagrant violation of international laws and conventions.” It did not respond to the Israeli claims.

The attack comes more than a year into a cease-fire between Israel and Hamas that has not brought an end to Israeli military strikes in Gaza and that has done little to alleviate the dire and deadly living conditions for the enclave’s two million residents.

Shortly after midnight, at least two missiles hit the roof of a four-story building in the hospital complex, penetrating the third floor and exploding on the second floor in the surgery department, Naseem Hassan, a paramedic and ambulance driver who works at Nasser Hospital, said by telephone.

Israel has continued to carry out frequent strikes in Gaza despite a U.S.-brokered cease-fire that Israel and Hamas agreed to in October 2025. More than 1,600 people have been killed by Israeli fire during the cease-fire, according to Gaza’s health ministry, which does not distinguish between civilians and combatants in its death tolls.

Nasser Hospital has been struck by the Israeli military multiple times since the Hamas-led attack on Israel on Oct. 7, 2023, prompted a devastating Israeli military response in Gaza. More than 74,000 people have been killed in the war, according to the health ministry.

In August last year, 20 people — including medical staff, rescue workers, patients and journalists — were killed in a strike on the hospital that drew a rare admission of error from Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu of Israel, who called it a “tragic mishap.”

An earlier version of this article, using information from an emergency worker, said that Khaled Abu Anza had been killed and that he was the governor of Khan Younis. Mr. Abu Anza was among the dead, but he was not the governor. The emergency worker also said that guards and a disabled patient had been killed in the attack; it is unclear whether they had been killed.
