#104. European survey reveals 900m galaxies – and possibly secrets of ‘dark universe’

Unprecedented view of cosmos provided by Euclid mission throws up many more cases of gravitational lensing

A mission to create the largest ever 3D map of the universe has revealed more than 900m galaxies in its first major data release together with about 15,000 potential cases of a phenomenon that could help unlock the secrets of the “dark universe”.

Launched in July 2023, the European Space Agency’s Euclid mission promised to create the largest cosmic map ever made, encompassing a third of the sky and more than 10bn years of cosmic history.

The goal is to shed light on two of the biggest mysteries in the universe: the nature of dark energy and dark matter. These are thought to make up about 70% and 25% of the universe respectively.

Prof Andy Taylor, of the University of Edinburgh, who leads the UK science ground segment for the mission, said while there were bigger surveys, none had the detail and the information content of Euclid. By contrast the Hubble telescope has produced images of incredible resolution, but does not cover as large an area of the sky.

“The technological leap that’s Euclid is that we can keep that high resolution, but now we can basically map huge volumes of the sky, huge volumes of the universe,” Taylor said.

Now, the first major Euclid data release has landed, offering researchers an unprecedented view of the cosmos. “It’s the biggest high-resolution image of the universe we’ve ever had,” Taylor said.

Covering about 14% of the total area of sky that will be mapped by Euclid, the newly released data captures over 900m galaxies as well more than 60m potential quasars: blazing objects at the centre of distant galaxies, powered by supermassive black holes.

It also includes a tranche of candidates for what was previously considered a rare phenomenon: strong gravitational lensing. This is a magnification effect that occurs when one galaxy, often embedded in a galaxy cluster, sits in front of another, distant galaxy.

As Taylor notes, Einstein predicted the gravitational influence of the foreground galaxy would distort space-time, bending the light from the background galaxy and creating the appearance of bright arcs or rings. Crucially, the effect is significantly boosted by the presence of dark matter, which increases the mass and hence gravitational influence of a galaxy.

Taylor said that by studying strong gravitational lensing, scientists may be able to rule out different theories on the nature of dark matter. That is because the lensing is influenced by how this mysterious substance is distributed in a galaxy – that itself depends on its properties.

Stephen Serjeant, a professor of astronomy at the Open University, agreed. “With strong gravitational lensing, you find out how the dark matter is clumping and that’s very diagnostic of the type of matter that it is, because different types of matter can clump in very different ways,” he said.

“So if you’re looking for where the next breakthrough will be in the properties of dark matter and what dark matter actually is, there’s a good chance – I can’t say it’s certain – but there’s a good chance it might come from strong gravitational lensing.”

Taylor noted that while experts previously knew of about 1,000 cases of strong gravitational lensing, the Euclid data contains about 15,000 probable cases, with an estimated 170,000 expected to be discovered by the end of the mission.

A further release of data was expected to land next summer, Taylor added, which will include results on both the distribution of galaxies as well as weak gravitational lensing – subtle distortions in distant objects by dark matter in the foreground.

“We can use that pattern of distortions to actually make a map of where all the dark matter is in the universe,” said Taylor, adding that scientists could then work out its statistical properties and compare the results with different theories. “Depending on the type of dark matter, the type of dark energy, we’re expecting to see slightly different patterns on the sky.”

And with Euclid generating ever-more data, Taylor noted there was plenty to explore.

“It’s opening up that high resolution of the whole sky, the whole universe. So it just means there are plenty of objects that we’ll never have seen before, plenty of research phenomena,” he said. “No human eyeball is able to look at all of the Euclid data any more. It’s now too big.”

#103. Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The chief executive and co-founder of the AI chip-making giant is at the centre of a major scandal over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

On Thursday, Nvidia's board announced he had resigned effective immediately.

He will be replaced, for now, by Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress.

The tech giant is facing one of its biggest challenges, finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Nvidia's finance chief, Colette Kress, is taking on the chief executive role for an interim period while the company searches for a permanent replacement.

An industry veteran for more than 30 years, Kress previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She "fronts every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

Liu said Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chipmaking giant.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#102. Germany news: Police raid chapters of Gremium MC biker gang

Police in southwestern Germany launched major early-morning operations against a biker gang. The state of Baden-Württemberg is outlawing three of its chapters. Meanwhile, unions are planning more strikes. Follow DW.

Here's a roundup of news from or pertaining to Germany on November 12, 2026:

Germany's major IG Metall trade union has enabled the possibility of longer warning strikes amid difficult negotiations for industrial employees.

"The board of IG Metall has pave the way for 24-hour warning strikes in the metalworking and electrical industry," the union said on Thursday.

The union accuses employers of intransigence after three rounds of negotiations without clear progress.

On Wednesday, regional chapters appealed for authorization for full-day warning strikes — the last step before a full union vote and an indefinite labor dispute.

"This escalation is completely excessive and counterproductive," Udo Dinglreiter, the president of the employers' umbrella organization, Gesamtmetall, said. He said that hundreds of thousands of jobs could be at risk.

IG Metall's first chairperson Christiane Benner countered that "the employers want to turn this round of collective bargaining into a dispute over principles." Another union official said they were offering what amounted to a real-terms pay cut rather than seeking solutions.

IG Metall is calling for a 5% pay increase and other improved conditions for some 3.7 million workers starting in November, while employers have offered a one-off payment in 2027 coupled with a 1.3% increase starting in the summer of 2028.

The union estimates that around 464,000 employees took part in the initial wave of warning strikes that were limited to periods of hours, rather than full days.

Employers argue that they're treading a difficult line between trying not to cut jobs and meeting calls for improved pay amid widespread industrial sector layoffs in Germany in recent years.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

'Tis the season, already. Preparations are underway around Germany and in several cities, Christmas markets are opening to the public.

Some markets are already open in Essen and in Hamburg, Duisburg's opens on Thursday, and Cologne's follows a day later.

The coupons.de website has sought to track the planned prices for mulled wine or Glühwein as the markets open their doors nationwide.

The bad news? These prices are rising like so many others. The better news? The year-on-year increase is actually smaller than overall inflation figures, at around 1%.

On average, a cup is liable to set you back around €4.60 (roughly $5.10). Not entirely surprisingly, Munich tops the nationwide pricing chart, with the price peaking at €7. Other sites nationwide tend not to have the gall even to charge 6 euros.

Bargain hunters determined to get the best value mulled wine will need to venture deep into the center of Germany — hardly its core tourist hub — to find the lowest prices starting at around €3.50.

Deposits for the cup, the "Pfand," are also only going in one direction, and in the meantime range from €1 all the way up to €5. But there's a strong argument, particularly with the better designs or after a memorable trip, that this can be money well spent as a memento!

Police in the Emsland region in the northwest near the border to the Netherlands have reported a major fire in a complex of residential and commercial buildings in Papenburg.

The fire broke out on Wednesday afternoon, police said the following morning, causing damages worth "at least €10 million ($11 million)." Several hundred emergency services personnel were deployed.

"Based on current findings, the fire broke out in the sauna area of a gym facility on site, and quickly spread to further parts of the complex of buildings," police wrote.

Two men were in the sauna at the time and initially tried to douse the flames but without success.

Police said the buildings were evacuated as the fire spread, with some surrounding buildings also being cleared as a precaution because of possible smoke-related dangers.

Several people were injured, police said, including the two men in the sauna area suffering mild burns and suspected smoke inhalation sickness. They were treated in a nearby hospital but soon discharged.

"There were also injuries among the first responders," police wrote. "Four police officers, two firefighters and members of the rescue teams suffered mild injuries, particularly via smoke inhalation."

The fire was only extinguished late on Wednesday night, several hours after it started. The area is sealed off, with possible risks of damaged buildings collapsing. Police have also launched investigations into the fire's cause, but said that at present, the area was not safe for that work to start at full scale.

Germany's largest credit bureau Creditreform has published its "2026 Debtors' Atlas."

It finds that 10.16% of German adults, or 7.36 million people, exhibit at least the early warning signs of excessive debt. A year ago the ratio was 9.74%.

"The financial situation of private consumers has further deteriorated," Patrik-Ludwig Hantzsch, Creditreform's head of economic research, said. "In particular, the sharply increased living costs, energy costs and housing costs are straining expenditures."

Hantzsch said employment was also becoming an issue once more, saying "above all, well-paid jobs in manufacturing industries have been cut back in large numbers in recent months."

The report also highlights rising cases of signs of excessive debt prior to legal action being taken, such as payment reminders or warnings being sent, saying "consumers are taking on more credit-financed expenditures — increasingly also for smaller purchases — at simultaneously rising interest rates."

A larger share of men than women have excessive debts, perpetuating a longstanding trend.

People around the middle of their working lives, aged 30-39 and 40-49, are most likely to be affected — but the cohort with the fastest-rising share of people with excessive debts are young adults below the age of 30.

Bremen, Berlin, Saxony-Anhalt and North Rhine-Westphalia were among the states with the highest rates of excess debts, while the wealthy and fiscally conservative states of Bavaria and Baden-Württemberg had the lowest levels.

Germany's main DAX stock market index has cleared the 27,000-point mark for the first time in its history in morning trading.

It climbed by around 1.3% in order to pass the milestone.

A combination of some positive outlook data from major companies, not least engineering giant Siemens, and the falling oil prices of recent days have helped fuel the uptick.

Siemens' shares had risen by almost 5%, to just over €291 (around $323) each, helping to spur on the gains and making the company the morning's top performer.

Despite several years of low growth and recession in the German economy, the DAX, like many Western indices, is now trading twice as high as it was prior to the COVID pandemic.

Hundreds of police officers searched apartments and meeting sites for the biker group "Gremium MC" in the southwestern state of Baden-Württemberg early on Thursday.

The state interior ministry said the operation was part of a move to outlaw three sub-chapters of the group, the ones based in Böblingen and Leonberg, as well as the "Gremium MC Chapter Nomads Bosphorus Türkiye."

This last group was highlighted as a meaningful part of Gremium MC by security officials. It describes itself on Facebook as a great example that "people of different cultures and faiths can be strong together," featuring images of men posing with knives and other weapons.

Gremium MC prominently incorporates into its iconography the symbol of the Prussian military medal for exceptional valor, the Iron Cross, which was discontinued after World War I, reintroduced by Adolf Hitler during World War II, and then scrapped for good.

State Interior Minister Manuel Hagel was expected to make further comments later in the day in Ludwigsburg.

Gremium MC is deemed an "outlaw motorcycle gang" in Baden-Württemberg's latest security report, a term used for biker groups that do not recognize the state monopoly on the use of force and are often tied to organized crime.

"Extortion, drug dealing and pimping in particular tend to be part of their activities," state authorities say.

Baden-Württemberg counts 154 biker chapters in the state with around 2,000 members.

Founded in 1972 in Mannheim, various chapters of Gremium MC across the country have faced legal issues in recent years. In 2021, another chapter in the southwest, Gremium MC Southgate in Heidelberg, was outlawed on suspicion of organized crime activity.

Police in Hanover reported early on Thursday that three men were injured by an unknown driver in a car in the nearby town of Ronneberg.

One person sustained severe injuries in the incident late on Wednesday, police said.

Two of the wounded were taken to a nearby hospital while the other could be cared for at the scene.

Police said the driver fled the scene and that a search operation was ongoing. Authorities were also still investigating a potential motive for the apparent attack.

Today's updates start with a focus between the southwestern Kurpfalz and Swabian regions, where police have launched major morning raids against a biker gang.

The state Interior Ministry in Baden Württemberg has outlawed three of its chapters. Hundreds of police officers mobilized in the early hours of the morning.

The major IG Metall trade union is planning further strikes in the carmaking sector and other metalworking industries, with an announcement anticipated later in the day.

To the north, near Hanover, police report an apparent car attack injuring three men overnight.

And in the spirit of offering the public service journalism people are truly thirsting for, we'll have some info on the average and localized pricing of mulled wine or Glühwein at Christmas markets this year, as the early birds start to open their doors to visitors. (Yes, already!)

Stay tuned for updates on all this and anything else of note in or pertaining to Germany as the day progresses.

#101. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#99. Israel strikes Gaza hospital, says Hamas senior figure inside

Israel says Hamas was using the hospital as a military command center, while Palestinian officials report at least six people killed, including a surgeon. The strike comes amid an increasingly fragile ceasefire.

Israel said it had targeted a senior Hamas member in a Gaza  hospital in an overnight airstrike which reportedly killed six people.

According to the Israeli military, Muhammad Abu Ismail and other militants from the Islamist group had been operating a command center inside the Nasser hospital in Khan Yunis.

Gaza's civil defense service said at least six people were killed, including a surgeon and more than 20 were wounded. It said that parts of several floors were severely damaged or destroyed.

During war, hospitals are protected under international humanitarian law, although they can lose that protection if they are used for military purposes.

In a statement on Thursday, the Israeli military (IDF) said, without providing evidence, that Hamas was "operating within Nasser Hospital in Khan Yunis" and were using the complex "as a hub for military and terrorist activities against IDF troops and Israel."

The IDF identified the targeted senior Hamas member as Abu Ismail, adding he "was responsible for directing terrorist attacks against Israeli civilians & IDF troops and for holding numerous hostages captive throughout the war."

The IDF said the hospital was used for storing weapons, to conduct interrogation, operate command-and-control rooms as well as to shelter terrorists.

The Gaza Health Ministry said the Israeli airstrike hit the surgery department, killing six people including surgeon Rizk Abu Sharia.

News agency Reuters reported seeing footage from inside the hospital showing extensive damage with debris scattered around rooms and equipment.

Wounded people were also seen in the wreckage while several bodies were being carried away as firefighters worked to clear the scene.

Hamas has described the strike as a "fully-fledged war crime."

Since the beginning of the war in 2023, Israeli forces have attacked several hospitals in the Gaza Strip.

The war on Hamas in Gaza began in October 2023 after Hamas-led militants attacked southern Israel, killing around 1,200 people and taking 251 hostages.

Since then, more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza, according to the Gaza Health Ministry, whose figures are generally considered reliable by the international community.

More than 1.6 million residents of Gaza's 2.1-million population now live in tents or other makeshift shelters due to Israel's war in the enclave.

A ceasefire came into effect in October 2025, but attacks have continued since then. While Israel says Hamas is trying to regroup, the Palestinian militant group says it's committed to the ceasefire and that Israel is trying to undermine the agreement.

Hamas is recognized as a terrorist organization by the EU, the US and several Arab states.

#98. Poundland owner secures two extra weeks to ensure retail chain’s survival

US investment firm Gordon Brothers is ‘taking due care’ to consider if one of three bidders has enough funds

Poundland’s owner has said it is “taking due care” to consider if one of three bidders had enough funds to ensure its survival after the troubled budget retail chain secured two more weeks to find a deal.

The management of Poundland, which employs approximately 12,000 people and operates 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, won approval from the high court for an extension of legal protection from creditors on Thursday morning.

Poundland’s board said it had sought the extra time “with a view to identifying the best available prospects for shareholders, creditors and our people”.

It added: “Our stores remain open as usual during this period and we thank our colleagues, suppliers and customers for their incredible and continued support.”

The retail chain’s owner, the US investment firm Gordon Brothers, has been under pressure to accept a bid for the company that would save retail jobs and protect thousands of suppliers since Poundland filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators two weeks ago.

A spokesperson for Gordon Brothers said on Thursday: “We support the board’s decision to extend the legal moratorium over the company by a further 10 working days. This brings additional stability amid an ongoing sale process in the backdrop of increasing cost pressures and a difficult inflationary economic environment.

“With this decision, we are today able to confirm to Poundland employees that funds will be released to allow payroll to be made in full this month, while additional funds continue to be released to support Poundland’s ongoing trading.

“We are in discussions with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and we have also received several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business.

“While our preference remains a sale of the whole company, we are taking due care to ensure that any successful offer can provide the funds required to acquire the business and see it through to sustainable cash generation. We, the board, and all involved are working to secure a credible plan that delivers certainty, preserves value and protects as many jobs as possible.”

Gordon Brothers confirmed it had provided £85m of financing, understood to be via an asset-backed loan that Poundland has only partially used over the past year.

That financing is in addition to a £30m shareholder loan largely taken on by Gordon Brothers when it bought the business for a nominal £1 last year.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Gordon Brothers fought back after MPs on Wednesday accused the Poundland owner of extracting millions of pounds from the retailer when it was on a “knife-edge”.

Documents provided to MPs showed the finance firm had taken close to £20m in interest payments and various fees, including those relating to store closures since acquiring Poundland.

Gordon Brothers said it had provided finance for Poundland “when no one else was willing to finance the company – and has contributed substantial efforts to support a turnaround.

“Had we not, the company would have collapsed into insolvency in August 2025. Our efforts have continuously focused on the company’s value and its people, not prioritising one over the other.”

#97. On Dakar’s Packed Beaches, Africa Welcomes Its First Youth Olympics

The 2026 Youth Olympic Games are taking place in Senegal, where the beaches and streets are teeming with sports fans and fitness fanatics.

The 2026 Youth Olympic Games are taking place in Senegal, where the beaches and streets are teeming with sports fans and fitness fanatics.

Skateboarding in Ngor, a neighborhood of Dakar, Senegal’s coastal capital. Fitness and athletic prowess are all-important in Dakar. Credit...

Ruth Maclean and Ricci Shryock reported from the beaches of Dakar, Senegal, where it seemed as though they were the only ones not exercising.

Teenage athletes from all over the world have descended on Senegal this month to compete in Africa’s first ever Olympic events, the 2026 Youth Olympic Games.

And in Dakar, Senegal’s sports-crazy capital on Africa’s westernmost tip, the locals are ready.

Fitness and athletic prowess have long been all-important here in Dakar. On any given day, ordinary young people put some of the world’s most toned muscles to work, wrestling, skateboarding, surfing and bike riding.

Women toting goggles and foam noodles make their way down alleyways to the sea to take part in mass early-morning water aerobics classes with energetic coaches.

Groups of men do synchronized calisthenics on the city’s public beaches, hopping backward in low frog jumps and coating themselves in fine sand after sets of push-ups.

Weight lifters lift homemade barbells formed by pouring concrete into buckets and sticking a piece of metal into it to dry.

And every evening, crowds gather up and down the city’s main coastal road — the Corniche — to run, lunge, jump and use the many outdoor gyms that have opened in the city in the past decade.

On a recent evening on Koussoum Beach, hulking men tied traditional loincloths around their waists and between their legs. Then they circled each other, pawing at each other’s hands like cats and pulling each other to the ground.

Beach wrestling is making its Olympics debut at the Games, delighting many in Senegal, where traditional wrestling is wildly popular. Traditional Senegalese wrestling has been practiced for centuries here, and in recent years has become extremely popular and lucrative for top-tier competitors.

#94. Hegseth and advisers reprimanded by guard at Tomb of the Unknown Soldier

US Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth and a group he was with were reprimanded by a sentinel at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier at Arlington National Cemetery on Wednesday. The incident followed a wreath-laying ceremony marking Veterans Day.

Hegseth had joined the group for a photo and someone laughed, prompting the US Army sentinel guarding the tomb to shout. "All visitors will maintain an atmosphere of silence and respect at all times", the sentinel said.

#93. US envoys meet Putin as Zelenskyy says Starlink disrupted for Ukraine’s military again - Europe live

Restrictions to Starshield ‘directly affect our ability to intercept Russian jet powered drones’, Ukraine’s president says

Zelenskyy also repeated his earlier warning that Starshield restrictions are back on “in the temporary occupied parts of Ukraine,” and “directly affect our ability to intercept Russian jet powered drones” launched from these areas (10:08).

On other battlefield issues, Zelenskyy says Ukraine needs more support on air defence, including interceptors and fighter jets.

Vladimir Putin’s comments during his meeting with the two US envoys make it clear that getting a deal with Russia will not be easy.

During his talks with Witkoff and Kushner, the Russian president doubled down on his demand to retain the Russia-occupied Ukrainian territory and opposed any foreign peacekeeping troops in the country – a key part of Europe’s planned security guarantees for Kyiv.

Instead, he continues to push for broader talks on rolling back Europe’s post-1997 Nato security architecture, a move which Zelenskyy called “strange” and urged Europe to watch closely.

Putin’s proposal for “a humanitarian mission” in Kostyantynivka is likely to be seen by Europeans as merely a token gesture designed to distract from his otherwise hardline stance.

As our Russia expert, Pjotr Sauer, said after yesterday’s discussions: “Russia appears to be seeking to revive demands it presented to Nato in 2021,” with maximalist conditions on future enlargement and deployments.

The UK government has announced new sanctions on all of Russia’s top 10 oil producers ramping up pressure on Moscow over the Ukraine war.

The British prime minister Andy Burnham said he was unveiling “a sweep of stinging new sanctions to turn the screws on Russia’s oil revenues and drive forward an end to the Kremlin’s needless war” as European leaders met in Dublin for talks focused on the conflict.

Meanwhile, Finland’s military said on Thursday that its forces had detected “a strike” on a ⁠Russian warship in the Baltic Sea a day earlier but the security situation in the area ⁠remained stable.

It was ⁠not ​immediately clear if there were any casualties or what had caused the strike. Estonian media reported ⁠on Thursday that the vessel was a small Russian navy ship and that it later returned to St ⁠Petersburg.

“The Finnish Defence Forces detected... a strike on a Russian warship in ​the Gulf of Finland ‌area and has ‌monitored the development of the situation,” a spokesperson for the Nordic country’s ‌military said in an emailed statement to Reuters.

Finland is not under any immediate military threat, the spokesperson added. The defence forces declined to provide further details about the location or timing of the incident, or how it was detected.

Putin also reaffirmed Moscow’s opposition to allowing Western troops in Ukraine as part of any potential post-war peacekeeping solution – a key demand from Kyiv in any deal to end the fighting.

Moscow would see any foreign troops deployed in the country as a “legitimate target,” Putin told US envoys in Moscow, adding their deployment would mean “the continuation of war”.

Vladimir ⁠Putin has told US envoys that ⁠Russia was ⁠proposing ​a joint Russian-Ukrainian humanitarian mission to the ⁠city of Kostiantynivka in eastern Ukraine, the ⁠Interfax news agency reported.

Airlines have been reluctant to speak out about bomb threats for a host of reasons, but some leading figures in aviation have expressed unease at Europe’s response in last week’s flight disruption.

József Váradi, chief executive of Wizz Air, the Hungarian budget airline, said he met officials from the European Commission recently to “urge these people to ⁠act together and find ways of getting coordinated… But this is a very ‌hot potato because of ‌the sovereign nation state concept, and no country wants to concede on this matter.” ‌

Among numerous airlines targeted last week, Wizz received hoax bomb alerts for 14 flights, diverting two aircraft after emailed threats.

He told Reuters that, assuming the hoaxes could be due to hybrid warfare, Europe “needs to think about ‌this, how to level up responsibilities against a threat like this,” adding that geographically “we are probably closest to any of these hybrid warfare exposures than any of the western airlines.”

Speaking to an airline industry conference in London on Wednesday, the UK’s aviation regulator suggested that Britain’s industry had, more or less, kept calm and carried on while others had diverted flights.

Chief executive of the UK Civil Aviation Authority Rob Bishton said the UK had given a “proportionate” response to the threats, while parts of Europe showed a “different approach and a potentially different risk appetite .. and more caution taken for whatever reasons they thought were appropriate”.

He said the industry had “well-honed protocols” and the threat analysis by government was “a well-tried and tested model.”

“What you got from the UK side was a by the book, proportionate response.”

Bishton added: “Were we expecting things like this to happen – yes, it is not a surprise. It was a successfully executed assessment and the decisions made were correct.”

Some of the emails accounts used to sent messages were tracked back to data leaks in Taiwan and the Philippines, Italian media reported.

That must change for peace to be possible, and next weeks will be a decisive test for Russian willingness to stop this war.”

His remarks conclude the public session of the meeting, with the discussions continuing behind the closed doors.

#92. Kenya Starts Countdown to End of Ebola Outbreak

The milestone is further evidence that African countries have mostly succeeded in preventing community transmission and isolating the small number of cases that have come in from Congo.

Kenya began a countdown on Wednesday to declare the end of its Ebola outbreak.

The final patient who had tested positive for the virus was discharged from the hospital on Wednesday. And the Kenyan cabinet secretary for health, Aden Duale, said at a news conference that the last 11 people being monitored for Ebola symptoms had also been discharged after completing a 21-day quarantine period.

“For the first time since this outbreak began, Kenya has no patient under treatment and no contact in quarantine,” he said.

The milestone is further evidence that countries have succeeded in containing the small number of cases, most coming in from the Democratic Republic of Congo, where the outbreak continues to spread.

Since Kenya announced its first-ever Ebola case on Oct. 6, two people have died and nine have tested positive in the country. The initial case was a Kenyan man who returned from Congo and infected others before the virus was diagnosed and he was put in isolation. He died days later.

The Kenyan authorities say that all of those who subsequently became ill were in quarantine before they developed symptoms and became infectious. As a result, there have been no instances of what experts call community transmission, when a person passes on the virus outside a medical setting.

Kenya’s outbreak will be officially declared over on Dec. 23, or 42 days from when the last case was recorded, if there are no further positive Ebola tests, the health secretary said. In all, 135 people have been monitored for signs of the virus.

The current Ebola outbreak has killed more than 5,300 people since it was first declared a global health emergency by the World Health Organization in May. But so far, the fear that the epidemic might spread unchecked across Africa has not materialized.

Uganda has recorded 20 cases and three deaths, but the outbreak was declared over in the country by August. The authorities in Nigeria last month identified a case in the capital, Abuja.

Individuals with Ebola have since been medically evacuated to several European countries. Two Americans who worked for humanitarian organizations involved in the Ebola response have also tested positive. Both were treated outside of the United States and have recovered.

Several countries have instituted travel restrictions to prevent the spread of the virus. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention instituted a requirement in November that anyone traveling by air into the United States must sign a document confirming that they have not been in Congo in the past 21 days before boarding their flight.

Kenya has also announced new rules for its citizens returning from Congo, including 21 days of quarantine and monitoring, Mr. Duale said. He gave no details of whether the quarantine would take place at home or in a medical facility.

Matthew Mpoke Bigg is the East Africa bureau chief for The New York Times, based in Nairobi, Kenya.

#90. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#88. ‘Tourists hate themselves’ – which is why Tasmania is telling them to stay away

Island state declares it ‘is not really for tourists’ – but is the marketing strategy just a cheeky piece of reverse psychology?

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Research shows “tourists hate themselves” – which is exactly why the marketing flacks at Tourism Tasmania are telling them to stay away from the island state.

Its new campaign declares Tasmania is “not really for tourists”, enlisting former SBS newsreader Lee Lin Chin to help sell the tongue-in-cheek campaign, based on the idea that Australians prefer identifying as a “traveller” rather than a “tourist”.

Its centrepiece is a short video of a man being pointed to the back of an endless queue for a “world famous” pastry. He eventually abandons the line to find solitude in the Tasmanian outdoors.

Chin, who lives mostly offline without a mobile phone, also fronts videos exploring the state’s walking trails on her visit.

Tourism Tasmania’s CEO, Sarah Kingston Clark, said the campaign was deliberately wry rather than anti-visitor, to reflect the “tensions” of modern travel.

Those tensions play out in the research, suggested Prof Can-Seng Ooi, a professor of cultural and heritage tourism at the University of Tasmania.

“Studies have shown that tourists hate themselves,” he said. “They want to avoid each other.”

Ooi identified stressed people yearning for “quietness” as the campaign’s target audience, especially those living in urban cities on the mainland.

“It taps into the type of tourist people want to be,” Ooi said. He added that people prefer to view themselves as “travellers” because of the negative connotation of “tourist”.

Ooi spent 20 years living in Scandinavia, researching how tourism, culture and heritage contributes to the development of regional destinations.

“The message is very Scandinavian, even the imagery: the sauna, the vinyl records, the books and so on,” Ooi said. “Instead of the cold north, we are talking about the cold south … it’s a quiet place, time slows down.”

Char-lee McLennan, an associate professor from QUT’s school of management, said the campaign used “an element of reverse psychology” that could benefit regional economies by steering visitors beyond established hotspots.

Prof David Waller, a marketing expert at the University of Technology Sydney, said Tasmania’s “Come down for air” theme was less “anti-tourist” and “more anti-overcrowded, line-joining, tourist destinations”.

“Lee Lin Chin is adding humour to the message,” he said. “Lee Lin Chin is an icon – intelligent, avant garde and [she has a] dry sense of humour.”

But McLennan said the campaign’s success depends on whether the real experience of visiting Tasmania lives up to its promise as well as delivering meaningful economic benefits to regional communities.

#87. Nvidia Scandal Unveils a Fierce Spy War Raging Behind Trump and Xi’s Diplomacy

That the United States suspected Nvidia’s leader was a Chinese spy target underscores the deep distrust between the two powers, even as their leaders try to stabilize ties.

As President Trump and China’s leader, Xi Jinping, prepare to meet next week to extend a fragile trade truce, a fresh espionage scandal has shown that behind their diplomacy is a fierce intelligence rivalry.

At its center is Jensen Huang, the Nvidia leader and an influential ally of Mr. Trump’s, who has acknowledged having an affair with a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for Beijing. China has denied the espionage allegation.

Under Mr. Xi, the Chinese Communist Party has built a more sophisticated and assertive spy service that regularly highlights the importance of technological security and of threats posed by the United States. At the same time, the Central Intelligence Agency has said that Chinese companies are a legitimate target for espionage in areas like artificial intelligence, semiconductors and biotechnology.

Nvidia’s computer chips power the global A.I. boom and sit at the center of Washington’s efforts to keep that technology out of China’s hands. That makes Mr. Huang, who has lobbied Mr. Trump to ease restrictions on the export of advanced chips to China, a prime target, analysts say.

“It is not at all surprising, given the centrality of Nvidia to U.S.-China tech competition, and the importance of tech competition in the overall U.S.-China rivalry, that someone like him would be a top target for Chinese intelligence collection,” said Sheena Chestnut Greitens, an expert on Chinese security at the University of Texas at Austin.

#85. ‘He is never leaving the house again’: mother reunites with son missing for six days in Victorian bushland

William, 17, was reported missing after straying from his camp near Walhalla in Victoria’s Gippsland region

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A teenager has shared an emotional reunion with his mother after being found alive following almost a week lost in dense bushland.

Police air wing found William, 17, whose surname has not been shared, laying down in a creek near Walhalla, 130km east of Melbourne, in Victoria, on Thursday morning.

Rescue teams had been scouring bushland from the air when they spotted the 17-year-old.

He was first reported missing after straying from his camp near Walhalla last Friday.

Officers were winched down to the site after spotting the teen, before bringing him back to reunite him with his mother, Elizabeth.

A Victoria police video recorded from the air shows William being located in bushland.

Later, the moment his mother first saw her son since he went missing was also captured on video. William was sitting inside the police helicopter in shorts and workboots.

He appeared to smile before embracing his mother in a warm hug as officers looked on.

“I am over the moon. He’s alive,” she told reporters at the scene. “He is never leaving the house again. He is not going anywhere.”

William was taken to hospital for monitoring.

The teenager had been dropped off by his mother for the weekend to camp with his friend on Friday. It is believed he walked a short distance back to the drop-off location to retrieve further camping equipment.

When William failed to return shortly after, his friend searched the area but found no trace of the teen.

He was wearing only shorts and work boots when he went missing, with a mobile phone, food and water left behind.

Police said he was last seen near Stoney Creek Road, between Cowwarr and Walhalla, on 6 November at about 4pm.

On Wednesday, investigators appealed to hunters, campers and landowners to come forward with any information after a hunting camera was found in the search area.

Emergency service workers, including the dog and rescue squad, had been searching the dense bushland since last Friday.

#84. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#82. NSW’s latest news Instagram account has an unlikely group behind it: the Labor party

Expert warns of ‘huge loophole in political communication laws’ as state government among parties presenting accounts as unaffiliated news sources

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

“Breaking,” a reel from the Instagram account nswnewsroom reads. “Another Liberal MP quits, almost a third now gone.”

The post looks like it could have been made by a media outlet. It cites the source as the Daily Telegraph and Jordan Lane, a New South Wales MP, who is the sixth sitting Liberal member to announce they won’t re-contest their seat at the March 2027 election.

But the account’s bio tells a different story: it was authorised by the NSW Labor state secretary.

The account, which first posted two weeks ago and says “keep up to date with with [sic] news from NSW govt” in its bio, is part of an increasingly common tactic where Australian political parties set up accounts that appear at first glance to be unaffiliated news sources. The accounts feature different names and imagery without prominent party branding, to promote specific messages or highlight their own side.

Andrew Hughes, an expert in political marketing at the Australian National University, said these types of accounts take advantage of a “huge loophole in our political communication laws in Australia when it comes to digital and social media communications”.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

“More and more of us get our news and information primarily from digital and social media,” said Hughes.

“There’s more and more potential for that information to be used against voters who aren’t engaged with politics or the news to a really good level of detail to spot disinformation and misinformation,” he said.

As unpaid content, the posts are not subject to the same authorisation requirements as paid advertisements at state and federal level. In NSW, an authorisation in the account profile or a website directly linked to that post is acceptable. The nswnewsroom account added its authorisation after reporting by the Sydney Morning Herald.

A spokesperson for the NSW government referred Guardian Australia to a statement shared with the paper, which said the page was “a Labor-affiliated social media page, consistent with the practices of most major political parties and a number of minor parties across Australia”.

In Victoria a similar account, vicnewstoday, was set up ahead of this month’s Victorian state election. While the account bio contains an authorisation by Victorian Labor HQ, the posts, including videos of Ben Carroll and ministers at press conferences, do not. In one, captioned “BREAKING: Liberals did a deal with ‘the most anti-worker party’”, Carroll criticises a preference deal between the Liberals and One Nation.

In late October, the Labor party launched a page titled “Don’t Risk It Vic”, promoting posts that raise concerns about an alliance between the Liberal and One Nation parties.

Meta’s ad library reveals that page was the highest-spending political advertiser in the country last week, paying $67,138 to promote its posts between 31 October and 6 November.

A spokesperson for the Victorian Labor Party said social media was “an important way to communicate with Victorians where they are, particularly those who don’t engage with traditional media”.

“There is a lot at stake for Victoria. And everyone deserves to hear the facts about what a Liberal-One Nation government would mean for our health system, schools, police and cost-of-living.”

Monica Attard, former Media Watch host, now professor of journalism practice at the University of Technology, Sydney, said in an age where social media was “awash with deepfakes”, it was “really important for consumers – and probably equally important for political parties – to disclose clearly and always”.

“I would think that the possibility of misuse or tampering would be relatively high.”

Ahead of the 2025 federal election, Guardian Australia uncovered a string of similar social media pages set up by Labor, the Coalition, Climate 200 and campaign group Advance.

All pages carried basic authorisation messages from their creators, but the page names and profile pictures did not immediately denote the source. Labor state divisions in NSW and Western Australia set up at least two anti-Peter Dutton pages, with posts raising doubts about health spending and WA’s GST deal under a Coalition government.

The Liberal party launched a page called “Teals Revealed”, scrutinising the voting record of Climate 200-backed independents, while Climate 200, in turn, set up “Independent News”, to promote news about crossbench MPs.

#81. Egyptian journalist's retrial adjourned as UN warns of 'assaults on press freedom'

Lina Attalah has said Egypt is "trying to limit the already-limited space" for independent media

A criminal court in Cairo has postponed the retrial of the prominent Egyptian journalist Lina Attalah, whose case has drawn international condemnation.

Wednesday's opening hearing was adjourned until 18 November after defence lawyers representing the editor-in-chief of the news outlet Mada Masr requested a new judge.

The UN has warned of "widening assaults on press freedom by Egyptian authorities, including the targeting, persecution and harassment of independent journalists".

Attalah told the BBC that a recent crackdown was meant to "send a chilling message" to "the very few newsrooms that are left".

She warned: "Everybody should be careful, everybody should be scared, everybody should be wondering if they are next."

Set up 13 years ago, Mada Masr became Egypt's leading independent news site with Attalah as its editor-in-chief and co-founder.

Last month, the New Cairo Misdemeanours Court convicted her of running a website without a licence - which she said she had tried in vain for years to obtain. She was issued a hefty fine of 3 million Egyptian pounds ($56,000; Â£42,000) and Mada Masr was ordered to shut down.

After that trial, Attalah found that a court had previously given her a one-year prison sentence, without her knowledge, for "spreading false news" - a charge often used against journalists and dissidents - as well as operating a website without a licence. It was issued in absentia in 2024, even though she was in the country at the time. Attalah is now being retried in that case by the same judge.

At the opening session, Attalah's defence team requested the judge's recusal, arguing that he had "already formed a conviction in mind against the defendant", Mada Masr cited its lawyer Hassan al-Azhar as saying, external. The judge went into recess before adjourning proceedings for a week to allow the court to consider the defence's request, he added.

Mada Masr also said Attalah's appeal against last month's conviction was scheduled to take place on 25 November.

Egypt has largely stifled freedom of the press, ranking 169th out of 180 countries for press freedom, according to Reporters Without Borders' 2026 analysis. The group says that at least 26 journalists who were arrested in recent years remain in detention.

While most newspapers and television stations now keep to government lines, Mada Masr remained a rare, independent and investigative news source in Egypt. However, since 2017 the site could only be viewed from outside the country.

It had reported on alleged corruption and human rights abuses, and provided deep coverage of politics and economics, as well as regional upheaval and wars in Libya, Sudan and Gaza.

In her BBC interview on Sunday, Attalah insisted that the factual reporting and professional standards of Mada Masr had an important role to play in Egyptian society, which could benefit the government.

"It's much better if you have a little space left to have it open for professional media organisations who do rigorous reporting, because if we disappear, then you make the space for conspiracies and disinformation," she said.

"People need to know, people need to consume information and you're shooting yourself in the foot if you're killing the newsrooms that do it properly."

In September, six journalists with the independent fact-checking platform, Matsda2sh - constituting its entire newsroom - were arrested in home raids by the Egyptian authorities and held incommunicado for nearly a week.

Six journalists working for the independent fact-checking platform Matsda2sh are facing terrorism charges

They have since been charged with joining the Muslim Brotherhood and financing terrorism, and their lawyers report that several were beaten and electrocuted in detention. They say that no evidence has been presented linking them to the Brotherhood.

Egypt's former president Mohammed Morsi â who was backed by the Muslim Brotherhood â was removed from power by the military, led by Abdul Fattah al-Sisi, in July 2013 after widespread protests. Under Sisi, who became president the following year, the Islamist movement was classed as a terrorist organisation, leading to a widespread clampdown.

On Tuesday, a spokesperson for the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights, Thameen Al-Kheetan, called for the Matsda2sh reporters to be released, with all charges dropped.

He also said the case against Attalah "raises fresh concerns regarding due process and fair trial".

"We call on Egypt to end all forms of harassment of journalists and media outlets, and to respect and fulfil the right to freedom of expression, including the right to information," Al-Kheetan said.

"The steps taken to suppress the continued work of both Matsda2sh and Mada Masr must be overturned. The people of Egypt have a right to read and hear views expressed through these fora on issues of legitimate public concern."

Attalah has suggested the authorities might again be trying to silence independent voices at a time of economic strain in Egypt and regional instability.

It has been widely noted that recent legal action against journalists comes after the US State Department waived human rights conditions on $320m (Â£240m) in its foreign military funding for Egypt in September. This allowed the funds to be released as part of Washington's annual $1.3bn military aid package.

The BBC asked US Secretary of State Marco Rubio whether he had raised the recent crackdown on independent journalism with Egypt's Foreign Minister, Badr Abdelatty, at their meeting about regional tensions in Paris on Monday.

"We didn't discuss it today," Rubio said. "Butâ¦ like anything else in foreign policy, there's always a balancing act to further the national interest of the United States. And in the end, Egypt has been a reliable and good defence partner of the United States."

"That doesn't mean you agree with everything another government does," he went on, adding that he was not "opining about their journalists".

#80. Alexander Darchiev, Russia’s Ambassador to the U.S., Dies at 66

The death of Alexander Darchiev was announced by Russia’s foreign ministry. He was appointed to his post in Washington in 2025, as relations between the Kremlin and the White House warmed.

Russia’s ambassador to the United States, Alexander Darchiev, who served during a rapprochement between the Kremlin and Washington, has died, the Russian foreign ministry said Wednesday. He was 66.

Mr. Darchiev died in Moscow of a “chronic illness,” Maria Zakharova, spokeswoman for the ministry, said in a statement published by Tass, the Russian state news agency.

“We have lost an outstanding diplomat, an experienced leader, a kind colleague and a mentor,” the ministry said in its statement. It described Mr. Darchiev as a “distinguished expert on the United States” who had “served in one of the most demanding areas of Russian foreign policy.”

Mr. Darchiev had been in the diplomatic service since 1992, and he was appointed as ambassador to the United States by President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia in March 2025.

His appointment came as President Trump sought to broker a cease-fire agreement to halt Russia’s war against Ukraine, which began in 2022. Mr. Darchiev’s term coincided with a thaw in relations between the Kremlin and Washington as U.S. policy shifted from staunch support for Ukraine under the Biden administration to pressure on Kyiv to make a deal with Russia.

In February last year, Mr. Darchiev led a Russian delegation meeting with U.S. officials in Istanbul about the operations of the two nations’ diplomatic missions. At the time he was director of the North Atlantic Department, a section of the Russian foreign ministry that oversees relations with Canada, Ireland, Britain and the United States.

“The dialogue on irritants has been ongoing, and certain improvements have been achieved,” Mr. Darchiev said last year in an interview with the Russian newspaper Kommersant in which he discussed frictions between the United States and Russia.

But he said the United States was tying discussions on certain issues, like Russian access to seized diplomatic properties in the United States, “with a settlement in Ukraine that suits its interests,” which he objected to.

Talks on ending the war in Ukraine, which had been stalled for much of this year, resumed this week in Abu Dhabi, the United Arab Emirates, after a push by the Trump administration that began in September. The discussions are not expected to yield fruit anytime soon.

It is not clear if Mr. Darchiev was involved in those talks.

Mr. Darchiev, who was born in 1960, graduated from Moscow State University in 1983. Information about his survivors was not immediately available.

Mr. Darchiev’s appointment to Washington as ambassador to the United States was not his first stint in the country or the region. He served as Russia’s ambassador to Canada from 2014 to 2020 and worked at the Russian Embassy in the United States from 2005 to 2010, according to a biography on the embassy’s website.

In the intervening years, Mr. Darchiev directed the North America Department and, briefly, the foreign ministry’s North Atlantic department, before serving in what proved to be his final role.

Ephrat Livni is a Times reporter covering news around the world. She is based in New York.

#79. Iran: Exiled journalists still repressed by regime

For many independent reporters, leaving Iran does not mean fully escaping the grasp of the Iranian state. The regime uses family members, bank accounts and the property they left behind to apply pressure across borders.

In times of crisis, Iran's political system is looking for scapegoats — and independent journalists, even journalists who have been living in exile for a long time, are high on their list.

At the end of September, Iran's judiciary announced that it had confiscated assets belonging to individuals accused of "cooperation with hostile governments." The measure is part of a previously announced plan targeting around 400 people living abroad, including journalists, activists, artists and athletes, whose assets are to be seized.

"The only thing I own in my parents' house in Iran is a bookshelf," says Amir Soltanzadeh. The exiled freelance journalist, who occasionally works for Germany's international broadcaster DW, discovered his name on a government list, which Iranian state media had labeled "traitors to the homeland."  His assets were to be seized. In an online post earlier this year, he published a photo of his book collection and wrote: "This library is my only asset in Iran. If you enjoy reading books, go ahead and confiscate them."

Soltanzadeh said that his bank account in Iran was also frozen. It contained the equivalent of around €500 ($555), which he used, among other things, to buy his family members gifts and flowers for special occasions. He noted it was "unjust" to list him as a traitor.

"I have not acted against Iran," he stressed. "Every position I have taken was ultimately aimed at improving the lives of people in Iran."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Some of his relatives, he said, have cut ties with his family because of his journalistic work.

"When people hear about a journalist working abroad, some who adopt the government's narrative automatically equate that person with a spy. They see a journalist as someone who is paid to gather information," said Soltanzadeh.

This pressure, according to the exiled journalist, eventually extended to his father's workplace.

"My father lost his job because of me. And because of me, my mother lives in constant anxiety — every time the phone rings, she has to wonder who is on the other end."

For several months, Soltanzadeh also cut all direct contact with his family to keep them safe, and only communicated with them through an intermediary.

Pressure against independent journalists is a part of a recurring pattern in the Islamic Republic. In times of political tension, Western media outlets and journalists are often portrayed as agents of hostile intelligence services such as the CIA or Mossad, or mouthpieces spreading subversive propaganda.

The authorities have many tools to spy on reporters, including hacking their social media accounts and intercepting conversations that are later used as evidence against their contacts inside Iran.

Such forms of repression also affect which information ends up reaching the public.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A journalist in Iran, who wanted to stay anonymous, says that reporting on detainees, state executions and the treatment of protesters comes with major risks and restrictions.

"Journalists can only write on their personal pages in a very limited and censored way," the reporter said. "In their reports, they are required to follow the government's official narrative."

Iran-based journalists have to weigh potential legal consequences of their work. This, in turn, constrains their ability to conduct independent research, maintain contact with sources and pursue sensitive topics.

Article 24 of Iran's constitution guarantees freedom of the press. However, the Press Law has been amended and expanded several times over the past four decades. It requires media professionals, among other things, not to endanger the Islamic Republic, not to insult the clergy or the Supreme Leader, and not to disseminate false information. These broadly worded provisions give authorities significant room to interfere in the work of the media.

Many journalists are forced to leave the country due to the constant pressure and the risk of being targeted by the authorities.

"Because of my journalistic work, I had to go into exile," says Omid Barin, a freelance journalist who also occasionally contributes to DW.

Barin's reporting focuses on issues such as human rights violations and the position of ethnic minorities in Iran. The pressure "has undoubtedly caused me to be much more cautious and sensitive when communicating with sources inside the country, to avoid putting them in danger," he told DW.

While Barin chooses not to provide details on the threats he faces, citing the risk of backlash on the people close to him, he says the core message behind them is clear: Someone in Iran would like him to either abandon journalism or steer away from sensitive topics.

Even while living abroad, Barin is forced to take additional precautions in his daily life and pay close attention to his personal security due to his work for media outlets that the Iranian regime considers "hostile."

#77. African writing award probe finds 'no evidence' winning story was AI generated

Simbarashe Kundizeza, who lives in Harare, says he was inspired by a poem about fish tanks to write a story about his family struggles

The Caine Prize for African Writing has said that after an internal review it has found no evidence that Zimbabwean writer Simbarashe Kundizeza had used generative AI to write his winning short story and confirmed him as the winner of this year's Â£10,000 ($13,000) award.

Ellah Wakatama, chair of the Caine Prize's board of trustees, said the matter had been investigated following an article in US magazine The Atlantic with the headline, Yet Another Literary Prizewinner Seems to Be AI.

"From our detailed investigation, we feel the evidence is clear: The Blue Fighter was human written," she said in a video posted on X.

The accusation had caused a social media uproar and Kundizeza had denied the allegation.

The Atlantic article by journalist Vauhini Vara alleged that AI-detection software Pangram had shown his work about a boy whose family falls on hard times was entirely AI generated.

Her allegations followed rows over other literary prizes this year, in particular the Commonwealth Short Story Prize, which was won by Trinidadian writer Jamir Nazir despite allegations that he had used AI, and the withdrawal of Canadian-Haitian author ThÃ©lyson OrÃ©lien from France's top literary award, the Goncourt, over allegations he used AI for his first novel, which he denies.

Following the publication of her article last month, Vara had told BBC's Front Row programme that every other previous Caine Prize winning story she had put through Pangram "came out as 100% human".

The backlash on social media included comments from African writers with suggestions that such accusations are primarily being made against non-white writers.

Zimbabwean author denies using AI to write award-winning story

Wakatama said the Caine Prize organisation had considered Vara's allegations "carefully and fairly".

"We chose to take a human-centred approach to the investigation. Our review included interviews with the author, the 2026 judging panel, and the Shallow Tales Review, where the story was first published," she said.

"It also included independent and expert-led metadata analysis of drafts written by the author."

In a statement, the Caine Prize explained further that the analysis had been conducted by an independent cyber security and digital forensic investigator, external.

The day after winning the prestigious Caine Prize award earlier in October, Kundizeza had told the BBC he was inspired after hearing a poem read by South African Vangile Gantsho at an event in Ghana last year about her father's obsession with building fish tanks.

He said he had weaved that with fictionalised details from his own life around 2002 when Zimbabwe's economic troubles began.
