#17. Kenyan doomsday sect leader jailed for 200 years for murder of 190 children

Paul Mackenzie convicted over deaths of children and one adult after followers were ordered to starve themselves and their families

The leader of a Kenyan doomsday sect has been sentenced to 200 years in prison for the murders of 190 children and one adult in one of the world’s worst cult disasters in recent history.

The sentence, comprising 100 years for murder by starvation and 100 years by direct homicide, came two weeks after the court in Kenya’s coastal city of Mombasa found the cult leader guilty of the crimes.

Prosecutors said Paul Mackenzie and his Good News International church ran ​a Christian doomsday sect whose members were ordered to starve themselves and their children to death to go to heaven before the world ended.

His accomplices threatened and beat victims to ensure they followed his orders. The emaciated bodies were found scattered in shallow graves and dumped in latrines. Some had been buried alive.

Mackenzie was arrested in 2023 after police received a tipoff, and weeks later authorities began uncovering bodies in shallow graves in the Shakahola forest near the coast in south-eastern Kenya.

In total, more than 400 bodies have been recovered. Many were severely decomposed and only 26 could be identified.

Mackenzie denied murder charges, saying he did not force the victims to starve, but the court rejected that argument.

The Shakahola tragedy has gripped Kenya since the discovery of mass graves in the forest more than three years ago.

It raised questions about how Mackenzie was able to continue operating despite being on the radar of the authorities since 2017, when police searched his premises.

In 2019, authorities shut down Mackenzie’s church and he relocated ‌to the Shakahola forest, police said.

The case has also led to calls for tougher oversight of churches and other religious organisations in a country where religious faith plays a prominent role in public life.

Mackenzie, a former taxi driver, forbade cult members from sending their children to school and from going to hospital when they were ill, describing such institutions as satanic.

Cult members lived in makeshift homes of polythene sheeting and thatch that Mackenzie divided into areas with biblical names such as Jerusalem and Judea.

The plight of his followers began ​to emerge in March 2023, when a man told ​police his brother and wife had starved their children ​to death in the forest on Mackenzie’s orders.

Mackenzie was arrested but released on bail, after which relatives said he brought forward the date ​on which he told his followers the ‌world would end. Police rearrested him and ​started combing through the forest more systematically and began exhuming mass graves.

Last month’s judgment covered 191 deaths while investigations into more than 200 other deaths are continuing.

Justice Diana Kavedza-Mochache said in a hearing lasting more than eight hours that in some cases children were isolated and denied food while their parents watched.

Mackenzie faces other charges over the forest deaths, including those of terrorism, which he has denied.

Prosecutors said that while he was in detention he also directed the deaths of 52 people in Kwa Binzaro village, about 18 miles from Shakahola. Mackenzie has pleaded not guilty in that case.

#16. ICRC: We are currently classifying the highest number of armed conflicts since the Second World War

François Picard is pleased to welcome the President of the International Committee of the Red Cross, Mirjana Spoljaric. “You may consider war a human condition,” argues Spoljaric. “But brutality, barbarity, is not a human condition.” Her warning recalls the fundamental premise of the 1949 Geneva Conventions: even in war, there can be no lawless space, and certain red lines must never be crossed. Spoljaric warns that the international rules established to protect civilians are being progressively weakened amid proliferating armed conflicts, technological transformations and the retreat of diplomacy.

#15. She Is Rewriting Mexico’s Ballads, One Woman’s Story at a Time

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Against the backdrop of a centuries-old stone church in the Mexican city of Oaxaca, the singer Vivir Quintana raised her deep, husky voice above the blare of trumpets, trombones and the booming pulse of a tuba.

Behind her stood a brass band of young Indigenous girls in embroidered white blouses and brightly colored skirts. Hat in hand and fist raised, Ms. Quintana sang a corrido, a traditional Mexican folk ballad usually used to recount tales of revolutionaries, outlaws, folk heroes, and more recently, drug traffickers.

Corridos are typically sung by men, about men. Ms. Quintana sang a corrido she wrote based on the true story of a woman who fought back when her abusive partner tried to gouge out her eyes with a knife.

“I used to think that what united us women was our pain. Now I have realized it is love and resistance,” Ms. Quintana told the crowd in October during a women’s conference in Oaxaca called Decididas, drawing a roar of applause. “We must keep defending ourselves!”

On Thursday, Ms. Quintana will take the stage at the Latin Grammy Awards in Las Vegas alongside the Colombian pop superstar Juanes, with whom she is nominated for song of the year for “Humano.” It is her second Latin Grammy nomination.

#14. Israeli strike on Gaza hospital kills six Palestinians, health ministry says

Surgeon films damage in Nasser hospital in Gaza

Six people have been killed and about 30 injured in an Israeli air strike on Nasser hospital in the southern Gaza Strip, the Hamas-run health ministry says.

The hospital's director said two missiles hit a building containing surgical wards and intensive care units overnight, but that one of the missiles did not explode.

The health ministry said medical staff, patients and companions were killed, among them surgeon Dr Rizq Abu Shaira. It condemned the attack as a "flagrant violation of international laws and conventions".

The Israeli military said it targeted a commander of Hamas's armed wing and other fighters who had used the hospital as a "headquarters" to plan attacks on its forces.

Hamas said that Israel was spreading "false narratives" in a "blatant attempt to cover up a new war crime".

Under international law, hospitals have a protected status, but they have been repeatedly targeted during the Gaza war.

Israel accuses Hamas, which retains security control of parts of Gaza not occupied by Israeli troops, of cynically exploiting civilian infrastructure. It says the group stores weapons and runs command and control rooms in hospitals, which Hamas denies.

Israel does not allow international news organisations, including the BBC, into Gaza, making it difficult to verify what is happening in the territory.

Pictures showed extensive damage to an operating theatre, wards and a sleeping area for medics

The Nasser Medical Complex is located in the city of Khan Younis and is the largest hospital in southern Gaza, serving almost one million people.

In a video posted on social media, a paramedic from the Hamas-run Civil Defence agency who arrived at the building after the strike early on Thursday said a missile penetrated the five-storey structure and hit a room in the surgical department.

Other footage shared by activists showed thick smoke inside the building and extensive destruction on the second floor, where medical equipment and beds had been scattered by an explosion.

The hospital's director, Dr Ahmed al-Fara, later told a news conference that Israeli forces had fired two missiles at the al-Yasin surgery building but that only one exploded.

"What's happened in Nasser Medical Complex is targeting the health sector mainly. We are talking about doctors, we are talking about civilians, we are talking about patients," he said.

"Also, there is... huge damage [to] the infrastructure inside the hospital."

Footage filmed by a freelance journalist working for the BBC showed extensive damage to an operating theatre, wards and a sleeping area for medics, as well as hallways and lifts.

News agencies also published photos that appeared to show an unexploded missile.

Photographs appeared to show an unexploded missile inside Nasser hospital's surgical building

Dr Mohammed Qdeih, a general surgery specialist who was wounded in the head, said he and other medical staff had just finished work around 00:30 and had been preparing to go to sleep when there was an explosion.

"My whole body was trapped under the rubble. For a moment, I felt as if I was about to lose consciousness, or that I was about to die," he told the BBC.

"I started struggling to free myself and started calling out, 'Guys! Guys!' I began crawling, trying to pull my leg out from beneath the rubble. I managed to pull myself free. It was dark, there were no lights, nothing at all."

He said he had been lying in a bed near Dr Rizq Abu Shaira, who was killed. Another colleague suffered a broken leg.

"Dr Rizq was the kindest person in this department. He was very kind and always willing to help... And of course, he was a surgeon, one of the best among us."

He added: "Patients were killed as well. There were patients in the rooms next to us who were killed. A patient comes to the hospital seeking treatment and leaves carried on people's shoulders. I do not know what kind of humanity this is."

Gaza's health ministry said surgeon Dr Rizq Abu Shaira was among those killed

The Israeli military said in a statement that its "precise strike" had targeted the commander of Hamas's Khan Younis Brigade, who it named as Mohammed Abu Ismail, alongside other fighters who were "hiding inside" Nasser hospital.

"The terrorists were struck in order to remove the threat they posed after turning the Nasser hospital compound into a headquarters and operating from there to advance dozens of terrorist plots against our forces and restore the brigade's force," it alleged.

The military added that it had issued advance warnings before the strike and released an audio recording of what it said was one given to a senior Gaza health official. But Fara insisted the hospital had received no warning of an attack.

A Hamas statement rejected the Israeli military's accusations, calling them "a blatant attempt to justify a documented crime targeting a civilian medical facility whose function and purpose are well known".

"The nature of the targeted site and the casualties inflicted on patients, doctors and medical staff expose the falsity and weakness of this narrative," it said.

However, local activists circulated claims overnight that the strike may have targeted a senior Hamas security figure who was hiding inside the building.

Fara said this was the seventh time that Nasser hospital had been struck during the war. In August 2025, a double Israeli strike killed 22 Palestinians, including journalists and health workers.

Later on Thursday, two more Palestinians were reportedly killed in separate incidents in northern and central Gaza. Medics said one person was killed in an Israeli air strike on a car in Gaza City and that the second was killed by Israeli gunfire in Bureij refugee camp.

The Israeli military said it struck a "terrorist" in Deir al-Balah and another in Gaza City.

Israeli strikes on Gaza take place almost daily despite a US-brokered ceasefire agreement between Israel and Hamas in October last year.

More than 1,620 people have been killed in Gaza since then, according to the territory's health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza over the same period.

The humanitarian situation in Gaza also remains dire and has recently deteriorated further as a result of heavy rains last weekend that reportedly flooded tens of thousands of tents housing displaced families.

The Gaza war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,650 people have been killed in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#10. How African philosophies inspired a new approach to staging film festivals in Manchester

See My World, an event highlighting the pan-African thought via film, poetry and dance, builds on momentum sparked by local activists and creatives

African philosophies including exploring how lessons from the past can shape the present and near future have inspired a film festival coming to Manchester.

See My World, a day dedicated to independent creators from across the African diaspora in the north of England, will take place at the Contact theatre on 21 November.

The festival is part of See My World’s year-round programme showcasing pan-African arts, culture and events.

It reflects the determination among creatives and activists in Manchester to build on the legacy of the 1945 Pan-African Congress in Manchester – which connected luminaries of postwar independence movements with a shared vision of liberation – after last year’s 80th anniversary commemorations.

See My World’s CEO and artistic director, Tunde Adekoya, said this month’s film festival was taking inspiration from across the spectrum of pan-African thought, including the works of Kenyan writers John Mbiti, an Anglican theologian who died in 2019, and Ngũgĩ wa Thiong’o, the author of the groundbreaking 1986 book Decolonising the Mind who died last year.

Both writers rejected the colonial conceit that African thought was inferior, stressed the importance of community and saw reclaiming African ways of knowing as essential to a people’s self-understanding.

While Mbiti sought common ground between African religions and Christianity, Ngũgĩ was a Marxist. He argued colonialism was motivated by cultural and economic conquest, and that European powers used language as their sharpest instrument. Ngũgĩ famously abandoned English to write in Gikuyu, a Bantu language mainly spoken in Kenya and criticised the role missionary Christianity played in colonial rule.

Adekoya said: “It’s a day dedicated to pan-African and global majority films from the north of England with exclusive premieres of documentaries we’ve been working on, which weave together expressions of liberation through art forms like poetry and dance.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

“The whole idea is that it’s like a playground in search of strategies of liberation, using the festival as a way to explore different philosophies.

“John Mbiti explores the philosophy and spiritual understandings of time through the concept of Sasa na Zamani – in which the past informs everything that happens moving forward. That reflects how we constantly go back to pan-African histories to inform how we progress.”

Film-makers have been invited to submit films lasting up to 30 minutes from across genres, with no screening fees charged.

As well as submissions, the festival will feature documentaries produced by See My World. They include Homage, a “cinematic love letter to dance” that traces a culture through present-day portraits illustrating dance artists and archival material from Manchester, telling a story of joy, loss, migration, rhythm and resistance. Meanwhile, Poetic Justice explores how pan-African thought and Manchester’s poetry scene speak across decades.

Adekoya added: “The idea is that we would go against the norm of how film festivals are, because when we think about decolonisation, we think about how structures have been created to alienate and exclude communities.

“So we thought our film festival would look more at how we make film accessible and how stories that deserve to be seen get seen. Throughout the day we will have different activities, from exhibitions to explorations of our performances, discussions, Q&As, insights into directors’ processes, and workshops.

“We’ve been looking for literature and concepts that inform us and give us an understanding of who we truly are. And as works from thinkers like John Mbiti have come into our sphere, they have really emboldened us to think differently about our place in the world and what we do.”

See My World film festival runs from 2pm to 11pm on Saturday 21 November at Contact theatre, Oxford Road, Manchester.

#8. Millions of Ebola dollars going missing as virus spreads, medical charity warns

MSF says that many frontline workers do not have the equipment they need

Hundreds of millions of dollars meant to help tackle Ebola in the Democratic Republic of Congo are not reaching the intended recipients, a medical charity has warned.

A report from MÃ©decins Sans FrontiÃ¨res (MSF) said that while the outbreak remained "out of control", frontline workers were going unpaid, lacked proper training and were struggling with shortages of essential supplies.

It added that in some cases ambulances had run out of fuel.

More than 5,400 people have died and 11,000 infected with Ebola in DR Congo since the outbreak was declared in May.

This makes it the second-most deadly outbreak of the disease ever recorded.

MSF has urged the Congolese government and UN agencies to "strengthen oversight and accountability" to ensure money already disbursed is effectively utilised.

This means "ensuring response personnel are properly trained, paid, equipped, and supported, and directing resources towards areas where needs are greatest," it said.

The BBC has asked the Congolese government for comment.

MSF's warning adds to concerns raised by Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) last month, when it said that $700m (Â£527m) out of $962m disbursed to organisations implementing Ebola response was unaccounted for.

At the time, Africa CDC chief Jean Kaseya said it was too early to describe it as embezzlement, characterising the issue as a major transparency and financial reporting failure.

What is Ebola and why is stopping the latest outbreak so difficult?

In its report, external, the charity said: "Despite the vast amounts of money provided for the response, many frontline responders are left trying to contain the outbreak with empty hands and empty pockets."

It also mentioned Ebola monitoring "surveillance teams that exist on paper but are nowhere to be seen on the ground".

"Our teams are often left speechless by the dysfunctions they witness in many areas. Six months into the response, this situation is simply unacceptable," said MSF president Javid Abdelmoneim.

One important way of stopping Ebola from spreading is contact tracing - finding all those people who had come into contact with an infected person and putting them into isolation. "Despite the resources officially allocated to this critical activity, surveillance remains deeply problematic," MSF said, adding that two-thirds of deaths were occurring in communities not treatment centres.

According to British epidemiologist Neil Ferguson, more than half of the cases are going undetected, meaning the outbreak is likely to last another year, reports the Reuters news agency.

MSF also raised concerns over "excessive concentration" of efforts in certain areas at the expense of other areas where needs were more urgent.

It said the response was "too slow" in North Kivu province while Haut-UÃ©lÃ© remained "dramatically overlooked".

In both provinces, MSF observed that teams were "inadequately resourced to respond effectively" with health facilities lacking essential items such as water, chlorine or triage systems.

"This has turned some facilities into new centres of transmission, rather than places of care that contribute to containing the disease," MSF said.

The epicentre of the outbreak is in eastern DR Congo, which has been wracked by conflict for many decades, with some affected areas under rebel control.

There are very few good roads in the area, making it very hard to travel around.

The latest outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo species of the virus, has spread beyond DR Congo borders, with cases reported in Kenya, Uganda and Nigeria.

On Wednesday, the last person infected with Ebola in Kenya was discharged, marking a major milestone in efforts to contain the disease in the East African nation.

The Bundibugyo species is relatively rare and has no approved vaccine or treatment - though trials are under way.

#7. Collision of two trains in Egypt kills at least seven

President orders prompt investigation after one train derails in collision in al-Ayyat about 50km from Cairo

Seven people were killed and at least 125 injured when two trains collided on Sunday south of the Egyptian capital, Cairo, officials said.

The crash occurred in al-Ayyat district in Giza province, about 50km (30 miles) south of Cairo, when a train travelling to Fayoum province crashed into the rear of a train heading to the southern province of Aswan, according to the transport ministry.

An inspection indicated that the Cairo-Aswan train had been stationary on the same track, officials said.

The health ministry gave the casualty toll, adding more than 70 of the injured were discharged after receiving treatment.

Train derailments and crashes are common in Egypt, where the railway system has a history of mismanagement and poor maintenance of equipment. In recent years the government has announced modernisation initiatives to improve its railways.

Ayman Gamal, a resident of Kafr Girza in al-Ayyat where the crash occurred, said people rushed to the scene, pulled out survivors and called ambulances.

Gamal said he saw about 80 of the injured being taken by ambulances to hospital. Some passengers were trapped inside one of the carriages of the stationary train and died, he said.

Egypt’s president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said in a social media post that he had ordered a prompt investigation.

Outside al-Wasta central hospital in Beni Suef province, where some of the injured were taken for treatment, survivors said passengers were in shock and struggling to breathe because of all the dust that was kicked up. Nearby villagers eventually got them out, they said.

Farha Gamal, a survivor who was inside the Fayyoum train, said she saw some of the passengers who appeared dead and others were struggling to find their way out of the car. “There were other cars that were completely mangled,” she said in an interview.

Emergency teams and cranes were dispatched to the crash site to clear the wreckage and restore train services to their normal schedule as soon as possible, the Egyptian railway authority said.

Last September, a train driver and his assistant were injured after two freight trains collided in the same district.

Egypt’s deadliest train crash was in 2002 when more than 300 people were killed after a fire broke out on an overnight train journeying from Cairo to southern Egypt.

#6. Democrat Scott Colom makes history as Mississippi's first black senator since 19th Century

Why Democrat Scott Colomâs Senate win in Mississippi is so significant

Democrat Scott Colom has won the US Senate race in Mississippi, delivering a shock victory for the party in a deeply conservative state that has elected Republicans for decades.

Colom's victory makes him the first black person from Mississippi to be seated in the US Senate since Blanche Bruce served from 1875 to 1881.

Fewer than five thousand votes out of nearly a million counted separated him from his Republican opponent, the incumbent Cindy Hyde-Smith.

The result means the Democrats will hold 55 seats in the 100-member Senate, with a knife-edge race in Alaska still to be called.

Before Scott Colom, these two men were the last black US senators in Mississippi

Colom, 43, won with 49.2% of the vote. Hyde-Smith, who received 48.7%, congratulated Colom in a video posted on X on Wednesday evening and wished him well.

"While this was not the outcome we worked for or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters," she said.

The contest was not considered to be in play until the final weeks of the campaign, given President Donald Trump won the state in the 2024 election by more than 22 points.

Colom offered a victory speech on the campus of Tougaloo College, met by hundreds of cheering supporters standing side-by-side under the night sky.

A crowd of supporters turned out to cheer Colom's win

Excitement filled the air as he thanked them for the support over the days of waiting for a result since the 4 November election.

"They did not believe a moment like this was possible, but I kept the faith," Colom said.

"It's not something that I did y'all," he continued. "It's something we did."

"In Mississippi, it's local people that make the difference."

Dozens of people were gathered at the Mississippi HQ of the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP) in Jackson on Wednesday when Colom's victory was announced.

The room burst into deafening applause and cheers.

"This is an amazing result," said Leon Russell, chairman of the NAACP board.

"This is purely the work of particularly the black community," he continued. "There was not a whole lot of money, but there was a whole lot of organising.

Across the room, filled with people embracing, Amber Thomas sat, revelling in the victory.

"When you fight, you win," Thomas said. "Right now, I am overwhelmed with joy."

Colom supporters celebrate his win in Jackson, Mississippi

The last black senator in the state before Colom was Bruce who was chosen by the state legislature.

It has taken a century and a half for the state to choose a new one, this time by Mississippi voters.

In his campaign, Colom strove to engage Republican voters, even going to a Trump rally to speak to people in line outside.

Another key moment was his opponent telling voters to "pick another protein" when asked about surging beef prices, something Colom turned into a viral advert of his own., external

Republican Senator Cindy Hyde-Smith has been in the Senate since 2018

Colom appeared to a ride a national wave of resentment against Republicans.

Voter backlash against Trump's Republican party has been driven in large part by economic woes, blamed on his administration's tariffs and war with Iran, according to exit polls.

Colom, a local prosecutor, used the national mood to his advantage, highlighting his opponent's support for Trump's tariffs and giving her the nickname "Cindy High Prices".

The Democrat campaigned aggressively, visiting all of Mississippi's 82 counties.

Hyde-Smith's campaign assailed Colom as a puppet of billionaire liberal donor George Soros, whose family has helped fund the Democrat's political campaigns.

The 67-year-old Republican was criticised for a lack of public appearances.

Democrats have scored a series of major wins in these midterm elections, taking control of both the US House of Representatives and the Senate.

The party flipped Senate seats in Republican strongholds, including Texas, South Carolina, Kansas and Iowa.

#5. A.I. Weapons Need Data for Their Training. Ukraine Has a Lot to Offer.

The Ukrainian government has opened up a trove of five million battlefield videos to defense companies at home and abroad.

The forest, seen from a drone, appeared as a vast, monotonous landscape of black tree trunks, shadows and patches of snow.

To the human eye, it appeared deserted. But to an artificial intelligence system looking for people, it did not. The system put a red box around a tiny human form, probably a soldier lurking in the trees.

Behind this feat lay vast volumes of data. Just as ChatGPT and other A.I. chatbots must be exposed to how humans write, military A.I. systems must be exposed to how humans fight. In Ukraine, this happens through a huge trove of battlefield videos, audio and text data collected over nearly five years.

The war database is being used by about 30 Ukrainian defense companies to develop A.I. weapons. Ukraine is now opening this repository, which it calls Avenger Labs, to firms in allied countries, hoping to deepen security partnerships.

Ukraine is providing the data at no charge, but companies must give the Ukrainian military licenses to any software they develop. Under the first agreement to be reached, 12 British defense companies will gain access. Ukraine is in talks with other countries.

While there is no shortage of Western companies developing autonomous weapons, there is a shortage of the data needed to train them. Ukraine can offer more of it than any other potential partner.

#4. Lewis Hamilton visits Egypt’s Rafah crossing and Gaza aid facilities

Seven-time F1 world champion tours border area and logistics warehouses operated by Egyptian Red Crescent

Lewis Hamilton has visited Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with the Gaza Strip to meet organisations sending humanitarian aid to the territory.

The seven-time Formula One world champion has spoken out about conflict and humanitarian crises in the region, and has called for peace. He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire.

“I think we need to make sure we’re pushing to free Congo, free Sudan, free Syria, free Palestine. I think there’s a lot of peace that I’m hoping we get to at some stage,” Hamilton said in October.

A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

Israeli forces have continued to advance, expanding their control from 53% of the territory to at least 60%, an independent commission of UN experts said last month.

Airstrikes also continue, and Israeli fire along the advancing front has killed at least 196 Palestinians, including 18 women, 32 boys and 11 girls, the commission said.

Hamilton toured Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with Gaza before visiting logistics warehouses operated by the Egyptian Red Crescent, which facilitates the entry of aid to Gaza.

At the crossing, Hamilton greeted aid workers with “Assalamu alaikum” a widely used Arabic greeting meaning “peace be upon you.” He shook hands and briefly spoke to workers and officials.

Khaled Megawer, the governor of North Sinai, where Rafah is located, said the visit was intended to help Hamilton learn about Egypt’s efforts to send aid to Gaza.

The visit comes after Hamilton made his 400th race start on Sunday at the São Paulo Grand Prix at Interlagos.

The first black F1 driver, he made his debut with McLaren in 2007. He is only the second F1 driver to start 400 races after his former teammate Fernando Alonso, who achieved the milestone in 2024.

#3. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

#2. Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza cleared of using AI to write prize-winning story

After a month-long investigation it was concluded that Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which won the £10,000 Caine prize, was ‘human-written’

Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza has been cleared of using AI to write a prize-winning short story, after the prestigious Caine prize for African writers concluded that his entry was “human-written” after a month-long investigation.

Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which charts the declining fortunes of a family after the father invests in fish tanks, was announced as the winner of the £10,000 prize on 6 October.

Five days later, the Atlantic published an article titled: “Yet another literary prize winner seems to be AI,” in which author and journalist Vauhini Vara said AI-detection software Pangram, which itself uses AI, had deemed the story to be almost completely artificially generated.

Caine prize chair Ellah Wakatama said in a statement: “From our detailed investigation, we feel the evidence is clear: The Blue Fighter was human-written. We now look forward to awarding and celebrating Simbarashe Steyn Kundizeza … before the end of the year.”

She said: “When concerns were raised, we have a duty as an organisation to look at them carefully and fairly,” adding that the prize had taken a “human-centred” approach to its investigation.

“Our review included interviews with the author, the 2026 judging panel and The Shallow Tales Review, where the story was first published,” Wakatama said. “It also included independent and expert-led metadata analysis of drafts written by the author.”

Kundizeza, who had strongly denied using generative AI to write the story, said he was “relieved” that the allegations had been dismissed after the investigation. “It was not necessary in the first place, I am happy to put all this behind me and continue with my writing journey,” he said.

Vara’s article prompted outrage online, with many claiming that Black writers, and Africans in particular, were being unfairly singled out by allegations they were using AI.

Associated Press contributed to this report

#1. 'No light, no water, no heat': Ukrainians struggle with blackouts as Russia hits power grid

Watch: Life in the darkness during Ukraine's blackouts

Daryna Kulchytska is a mum under pressure. A single parent in a seventh floor flat in Kyiv trying to feed two lively kids and ensure they do their homework. But Daryna also faces the added strain of a deadline she can't even be certain of.

All she knows is soon the lights will go out, the sockets will lose power, the wi-fi will drop, and the taps will run dry. Tonight, the electricity has kept flowing an hour longer than advertised, but Daryna's still racing to serve dinner and finish her own work from home.

"The last few days it was only four or five hours we had power, but it's not at the same times. For two hours we have electricity, then for eight hours we don't."

As peace talks continue, with US envoys meeting the Russian and Ukrainian leaders on Thursday, Ukrainians are facing a rolling schedule of planned power cuts because of intensified Russian attacks on their energy grid. Ukraine has responded by striking Russian oil refineries.

Officials are concerned that life will quickly become unsustainable for millions of Ukrainians if power supplies cannot be maintained for at least four hours per day. Some privately warn that's close already, as some families are living with outages of up to 20 hours in Kyiv.

The power cuts were introduced earlier this year than last winter, and they're already lasting longer - making homes like Daryna's darker and colder. She's wrapped in layers of clothing and a heavy coat - the temperature is dropping, indoors and out.

Just before 7:30pm, there's a click and that's it - darkness until dawn.

The lift doesn't work, the water will stop flowing because electricity is needed to pump it to their flat. So the torches and LED candles come out and Daryna, her son Kyryll and daughter Olena gather for a card game of Uno before an early night.

Twelve-year-old Kyryll, speaking perfect English learned during two years living in the US, is frank about the disruption.

"There's no light, no water, no heat, no electricity. And no fun. All the fun got sucked out with the electricity," he says. "We have a lot of board games. We have a whole shelf, but we've played them like a hundred thousand times already."

Many families sit in the shadows as blackouts sweep entire neighbourhoods, smothering homes and streets in darkness.

"You look outside and sometimes you see nothing. You have a big city around but you see nothing because there's no light," Daryna says.

Kyiv has been worst hit - its metro trains and trolleybuses at times brought to a standstill. Nationwide, hundreds of thousands of Ukrainians have gone without power in recent weeks.

We saw why on a visit to one of Ukraine's largest power stations, barely standing after a recent ballistic missile strike - the most powerful and devastating attack of Russia's campaign against Ukrainian energy targets.

Damaged parts from the power plant lie scattered on the ground

The enormous plant - which we've been asked not to name for security reasons - has been reduced to a pile of twisted metal and broken pipes, its turbines buried in rubble, taking the site completely off line.

Volodymyr Kovalenko was on duty in the control room keeping the plant working when the missiles hit at 3am.

"It was a very powerful attack. We'd never experienced anything like it. We'd had strikes before â part of the roof had collapsed, it was pretty grim. But this time it was particularly severe."

Kovalenko, who followed in his parents' footsteps and has worked at the site for 25 years, says the damage is heartbreaking, though he's confident it can be repaired.

But the work is being hampered by air raids every 15-30 minutes â seven times during our four-hour visit. Each warning of drones and further missiles forces teams to down tools and dive for cover, though during our visit all were shot down or flew on towards other targets.

If there isn't time to reach the site's main shelter, the workers hide in the foundations of its towering chimneys.

"It's very difficult," says Vadym Bobyr, who is leading the recovery effort. "You've mentally prepared yourself to do a particular job, and thenâ¦ the air raid siren goes off, and you have to get out as quickly as possible."

The 39-year-old, who has worked here half his life, is dwarfed by the mangled ruins and daunted by the challenge of rebuilding the plant.

"It's emotionally difficult, because we've already repaired all this, got it running again, and now it's happened all over again. It hurts, but we keep working and getting things done."

As we speak there's another alert - an incoming Banderol cruise missile.

The workers dart through the maze of passages and pipework but there isn't time to reach a shelter - one of the plant's thickest walls will have to do for cover. The missile was eventually intercepted 5km (3 miles) away.

The mangled wreckage of the Ukrainian power plant is still smouldering

Energy workers are at enormous risk. Since August there have been almost daily attacks on power facilities, claiming several lives every week. And it's getting more dangerous.

On the edge of the site, with the latest threat over, a team returns to reinstating the power lines feeding the national grid. But they won't be carrying electricity from the shattered plant before the end of this winter, at least.

President Volodymyr Zelensky says Ukraine still has enough generating capacity to meet electricity demand - the problem is getting it to homes and businesses when so many power lines and substations have been hit. Russia is using "waiter drones" that cling to pylons and cables and go undetected until Russia decides to detonate them.

Maxim Timchenko, CEO of DTEK, Ukraine's largest private energy company, said Russia was already putting the degraded electricity grid under even greater pressure than last year, and that overstretched air defences were unable to protect all of Ukraine's critical infrastructure.

"What is different is the scale of attacks and the different types of weapons used in these attacks. This is what is the difference - they've doubled in power and in scale".

He said rebuilding the damaged power station would take at least until next summer, because workers under sustained attack were limited to only one or two productive hours per day.

"We are fighting for survival of not only the whole country but ordinary people, especially during winter," Timchenko says.

Volodymyr Kovalenko has worked at the power station for 25 years

Kovalenko echoes the sense of mission about trying to keep the lights on. "Energy is the foundation of everything - people's lives, the state. Nothing can function without it. I've dedicated my entire life to this".

Many are choosing to leave the capital, or Ukraine altogether, to avoid months of bitter cold. Daryna, Kyryll and Olena know the winter has barely begun.

"It's the worst feeling," Kyryll says. "When I get home from school, I'm tired. I lay in my bed. Bam! Air alarm. Bam! No lights. Bam! A lot of homework. Bam!"

When the children are in bed Daryna confides her thoughts of moving to somewhere safer, or warmer. She's torn by the desire to stay at home - close to friends and relatives - and the worry of how unbearable life may become.

The strain is damaging her mental health, she tells me, and she fears the impact on Kyryll and Olena, who suffers nightmares. Still, Daryna remains defiant despite the darkness.

"I don't want to live without light. But actually people in Ukraine say, OK, without light, but without Russians. Yes, we're scared, we're scared a lot. But better for us to be without power, but [also] without Russians."

Additional reporting by Anastasiia Levchenko, Kamila Hrabchuk and Daniel Wittenberg
